if and only if

P_{V}\!\left(s_{1}^{\sharp}\right)\!(\mathfrak{C}(\sharp a_{1}\sharp\dots\sharp a_{n},t))\geq P_{V}\!\left(s_{2}^{\sharp}\right)\!(\mathfrak{C}(\sharp a_{1}\sharp\dots\sharp a_{n},t)).

This means that
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
if and only if
s_{1}^{\sharp}\leq s_{2}^{\sharp}
.
∎

In view of Theorem
C.5.1
, we can extend the approximation algorithm
for the generative case from
[
PFBLM18
]

to the reactive case.
In order to do this, we need to also consider
the schedulers that are necessary for reactive systems.
Instead of deciding the faster-than relation,
we consider the time-bounded approximation problem,
which asks the following:
Given
\varepsilon>0
, a time bound
b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
and two SMDPs
U
and
V
, determine whether for all schedulers
\sigma

there exists a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(u_{0})(C)\geq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(v_{0})(C)-\varepsilon

(C.1)

for all time-bounded cylinders
C=\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t)
where
t\leq b
.

First we identify the kind of distributions
for which our algorithm will work.
Given a class
\mathcal{C}
of distributions,
we let
\mathrm{Convex}(\mathcal{C})
denote the closure of
\mathcal{C}

under convex combinations, and
\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C})
denote the closure of
\mathcal{C}

under both convex combinations and convolutions.

C.5.2
.

A class of distributions
\mathcal{C}

is
effective
if for any
\varepsilon>0
,

b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C})
,

\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mid\mu_{1}([0,t])\geq\mu_{2}([0,t])-\varepsilon\text{ and }t\leq b\}

is a semialgebraic set.

A semialgebraic set is essentially one that can be expressed in the first-order theory of the reals,
and hence membership in such a set can be decided by utilising the decidability of the first-order theory of reals
[
Tarski51
]
.
In addition to effectiveness,
we will also require residence-time distributions
to take some non-zero amount of time to fire.
This requirement is made precise by the following definition.

C.5.3
.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of distributions is
slow

if for any finite subset
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
there exists a computable function
\varepsilon:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to[0,1]

such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

and
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}\in\mathrm{Convex}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
we have

(\mu_{1}*\dots*\mu_{n})([0,t])\leq\varepsilon(n,t)

and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varepsilon(n,t)=0
.

It has been shown in
[
PFBLM18
]
that the class of uniform distributions
and the class of exponential distributions are both effective and slow.
The importance of the closure under convex combinations and convolutions
in Definition
C.5.2
is explained by the following lemma.

C.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of distributions,
and let
U
be a SMDP with residence-time distributions taken from
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s)(a_{1}\dots a_{n})\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C})

for any scheduler
\sigma
, state
s
, and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in L
.

Proof.

The lemma follows essentially from Proposition
C.4.8
.
For
n=1
we get

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s)(a)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\tau^{\sigma}(s,a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\rho(s)\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C}).

For
n>1
we get

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s)(a_{1}\dots a_{n})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\tau^{\sigma}(s,a)(s^{\prime})\cdot(\rho(s)*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s^{\prime})(a_{2}\dots a_{n})),

and since
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s^{\prime})(a_{2}\dots a_{n})\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C})
by induction hypothesis,
it follows that
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s)(a_{1}\dots a_{n})\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

Lemma
C.5.4
shows that, for fixed schedulers
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
,
we can decide whether
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(u)(C)\geq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(v)(C)

whenever
U
and
V
have effective residence-time distributions using the first-order theory of reals.

The following theorem shows that, again for fixed schedulers,
we can find an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that the probability of any time-bounded cylinder
with length greater than
N
is less than
\varepsilon
.
Therefore any such time-bounded cylinder trivially satisfies the inequality (
C.1
)
and can thus be disregarded.

C.5.5
[
PFBLM18
, Theorem 5]
.

Let
U
be a SMDP with slow residence-time distributions.
For any state
s\in S
,
\varepsilon>0
,
b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and scheduler
\sigma
,
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(s)(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},b))\leq\varepsilon

for all
n\geq N
.

All that is left now is to consider schedulers.
However, since we only need to consider time-bounded cylinders up to some finite length,
we can also represent a scheduler as a collection of finitely many probability distributions over the action labels.
Each such distribution can in turn be represented as a collection of real variables that must sum to no more than
1
.
Hence schedulers can also be represented in the first-order theory of reals.

C.5.6
.

Let
U
be a SMDP with slow residence-time distributions.
Then the time-bounded approximation problem is decidable.

Proof.

By Theorem
C.5.5
,
we can find some
N\in\mathbb{N}

such that
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(v_{0})(C)-\varepsilon\leq 0

for any scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}
and any time-bounded cylinder
bounded by
b
and of length
n\geq N
.
This means that for any such time-bounded cylinder,
we trivially have

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(u_{0})(C)\geq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(v_{0})(C)-\varepsilon

for any scheduler
\sigma
.
It is therefore enough to only consider time-bounded cylinders of length
n\leq N
.

Now let
\sigma
be a scheduler.
We can represent
\sigma
in the first-order theory of reals as follows.
For each state
s
and label
a
(recall there are finitely many of these),
let
x_{s,a}
be a real variable.
Then we interpret
x_{s,a}
to be the probability
\sigma(s)(a)
,
and we impose the constraint
\sum_{a\in L}x_{s,a}\leq 1
.
The whole time-bounded approximation problem
can therefore be encoded in the first-order theory of reals,
and is thus decidable.
∎

Compositionality

Next we introduce the notion of composition of SMDPs.
As argued in
[
SV04
]
, the style of synchronous CSP composition
is the most natural one to consider for reactive probabilistic systems,
so this is the one we will adopt.
However, we leave the composition of the residence-times as a parameter,
so that we can compare different kinds of composition.

C.6.1
.

A function
\star:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

is called a
residence-time composition function
if it is commutative,
i.e.
\star(\mu,\nu)=\star(\nu,\mu)
for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.

One example of such a composition function is when
\star
is a coupling,
which is a joint probability measure such that its marginals are
\mu
and
\nu
.
A simple special case of this is the product measure
\star(\mu,\nu)=\mu\times\nu
,
which is defined by
(\mu\times\nu)(B_{1}\times B_{2})=\mu(B_{1})\cdot\nu(B_{2})

for all Borel
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
.

In order to model the situation in which we want the composite system
only to take a transition when both components can take a transition,
it is natural to take the minimum of the two probabilities,
which corresponds to waiting for the slowest of the two.
In that case, we let

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}(t)=\min\{F_{\mu}(t),F_{\nu}(t)\},

and we call this
minimum composition
.
Likewise, if we only require one of the components to be able to take a transition,
then it is natural to take the maximum of the two probabilities by letting

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}(t)=\max\{F_{\mu}(t),F_{\nu}(t)\},

which we call
maximum composition
.
A special case of minimum composition is the composition on rates used in PEPA
[
hillston2005
]
,
and a special case of maximum composition is the composition on rates used in TIPP
[
gotz1993
]
.

Further knowledge about the processes that are being composed
lets one define more specific composition functions.
As an example, if we know that the components only have exponential distributions,
then we can define composition functions that work directly on the rates of the distributions.
If
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
and
F_{\nu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]
,
then one could for example let
\star(\mu,\nu)
be such that

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime}\right].

This corresponds to the composition on rates that is used in SPA
[
hermanns1998
]
,
and we will call it
product composition
.
Note that product composition is not given by the product measure.

C.6.2
.

Let
\star
be a residence-time composition function.
Then the
\star
-composition
of
U
and
V
, denoted by
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}V=(S,\tau,\rho)
, is given by

•

S=U\times V
,

•

\tau((u,v),a)((u^{\prime},v^{\prime}))=\tau_{U}(u,a)(u^{\prime})\cdot\tau_{V}(v,a)(v^{\prime})
for all
a\in L
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\allowbreak\in S
, and

•

\rho((u,v))=\star(\rho_{U}(u),\rho_{V}(v))
.

When considering the composite SMDP
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}V

of two SMDPs
U
and
V
,
we will also write
u\mathbin{\|_{\star}}v

to denote the composite state
(u,v)
of
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}V

where
u\in S_{U}
and
v\in S_{V}
.

Parallel Timing Anomalies

If we have two components
U
and
V
,
and we know that
U
is faster than
V
,
then if
V
is in parallel with some context
W
,
we would expect this composition to become faster when
we replace the component
V
with the component
U
.
However, sometimes this fails to happen,
and we will call such an occurrence a
parallel timing anomaly
.

In this section we show that parallel timing anomalies can occur
for some of the kinds of composition discussed in Section
C.6
.
We do this by giving different contexts
W
for the SMDPs
U
and
V

from Figure
C.4.1
,
for which it was shown in Example
C.4.9
that
U\preceq V
.
Our examples of parallel timing anomalies make no use of non-determinism or probabilistic branching,
thus showing that the parallel timing anomalies are caused
inherently by the timing behaviour of the SMDPs.
For ease of presentation, we let the set of labels
L

consist only of the label
a
in this section.

\mu^{\prime}

w_{0}
\nu^{\prime}

w_{1}
\eta^{\prime}

w_{2}
W
(1,a)
(1,a)
(1,a)

Figure C.6.1
:
For different instantiations of
\mu^{\prime}
,
\nu^{\prime}
, and
\eta^{\prime}
, the context
W
leads to parallel timing anomalies for product, minimum, and maximum rate composition, respectively.

Consider the two SMDPs
U
and
V
depicted in Figure
C.4.1
.
For the examples in this section,
let
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right]
,
F_{\nu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[0.5\right]
,
and let
\eta
be arbitrary.

C.6.3
Product composition.

Let
\star
be product composition and
let the context
(W,w_{0})
be given by Figure
C.6.1
,
where
F_{\mu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[10\right]
,
F_{\nu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[0.1\right]
and
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.
In
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
, the rates in the first two states will then be
20
and
0.05
,
and in
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
they will be
5
and
0.5
.
Consider the time-bounded cylinder
\mathfrak{C}(aa,2)
.
Then we see that

\mathbb{P}(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.09\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.30,

showing that
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W\not\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.
Hence we have a parallel timing anomaly.
What happens is that in the process
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
the probability of taking a transition before time
2

with rate
5
is already very close to
1
,
so the process
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
does not gain much by having a rate of
20
,
whereas in the next step,
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
gains a lot of probability by having a rate of
0.5

compared to the rate
0.05
of
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.

C.6.4
Minimum composition.

Let
\star
be minimum composition
and let the context
(W,w_{0})
be given by Figure
C.6.1
,
where
F_{\mu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[1\right]
,
F_{\nu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right]
, and
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.
The rates of
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
are then
1
and
0.5
,
whereas they are
0.5
and
2
in
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.40\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.51,

so
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W\not\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.
What happens in this example is that in the second step,

U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
has the same rate as
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
had in the first step.
This means that
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
must be proportionally faster in the second step.
However,
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
has a rate of
2
in the second step,
but
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
only had a rate of
1
in the first step.

C.6.5
Maximum composition.

Let
\star
be maximum composition and
let the context
(W,w_{0})
be given by Figure
C.6.1
,
where
F_{\mu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right]
,
F_{\nu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[1\right]
, and
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.

U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
then has rates
2
and
1
,
and
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
has rates
2
and
2
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.75\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(aa,2))\approx 0.91,

so
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W_{3}\not\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W_{3}
.
The reason for the timing anomaly in this case is clear:

V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
simply has a higher rate in each step than
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
does.

Avoiding Parallel Timing Anomalies

We have seen in the previous section that parallel timing anomalies can occur.
We now wish to understand what kind of contexts do not lead to timing anomalies.
In this section we assume that the set
L
of transition labels is a finite set.
Also, we fix a residence-time composition function
\star

and two additional SMDPs
(W,w_{0})=(S_{W},\tau_{W},\rho_{W})
and
(W^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0})=(S_{W^{\prime}},\tau_{W^{\prime}},\rho_{W^{\prime}})

which should be thought of as contexts.
Next we identify conditions on
(W,w_{0})
such that

U\preceq V
will imply
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.

We first give conditions that over-approximate the faster-than relation between the composite systems
by requiring that when
U
and
W
are put in parallel,
then the composite system is point-wise faster than
U
along all paths.
Likewise, we require that when
V
and
W
are put in parallel,
the composite system is point-wise slower than
V
along all paths.
If we already know that
U
is faster than
V
,
this will imply by transitivity that
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
is faster than
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
.
We have already seen in Example
C.4.9

that a process
U
need not be point-wise faster than
V
along all paths
in order for
U
to be faster than
V
.
However, by imposing this condition,
we do not need to compare convolutions of distributions,
but can compare the distributions directly.

We first introduce some terminology.
We will say that a SMDP
M
has a
deterministic Markov kernel

if for all states
s
and labels
a
,
there is at most one state
s^{\prime}
such that
\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime})>0
.

C.6.6
.

A
state path in
M
is a sequence of states
s_{1},s_{2},\dots

where for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a label
a\in L

such that
\tau(s_{i},a)(s_{i+1})>0
.
For a state path
\pi=s_{1},s_{2},\dots
, we let
\pi[i]=s_{i}
,

\pi|^{i}=s_{i},s_{i+1},\dots
,
\pi|_{i}=s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{i}
,
and we let
\Pi[M]
denote the set of all state paths in
M
.
For a state
s\in S
, we let
\Pi[s]=\{\pi\in\Pi[M]\mid\pi[1]=s\}

and we let
\Pi_{n}[s]=\{\pi|_{n}\mid\pi\in\Pi[s]\}
.

C.6.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say that
\star
is
n
-monotonic in
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}
,
written
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox^{n}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
if
W^{\prime}
has a deterministic Markov kernel and

•

F_{\rho(\pi_{U}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho_{U}(\pi_{U}[i])}(t)
and

F_{\rho_{V}(\pi_{V}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho(\pi_{V}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i])}(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
1\leq i\leq n
,

•

for all schedulers
\sigma_{U}
for
U
there exists a scheduler
\sigma_{U,W}
for
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
such that we have

\tau^{\sigma_{U,W}}(\pi_{U}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i],a)(\pi_{U}[i+1]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i+1])\geq\tau^{\sigma_{U}}_{U}(\pi_{U}[i],a)(\pi_{U}[i+1]),

and

•

for all schedulers
\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}
for
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W^{\prime}
there exists a scheduler
\sigma_{V}
for
V
such that we have

\tau^{\sigma_{V}}_{V}(\pi_{V}[i],a)(\pi_{V}[i+1])\geq\tau^{\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}}(\pi_{V}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i],a)(\pi_{V}[i+1]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i+1])

for all state paths
\pi_{U}\in\Pi_{n}[u_{0}]
,

\pi_{V}\in\Pi_{n}[v_{0}]
,
\pi_{W}\in\Pi_{n}[w_{0}]
, and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{n}[w^{\prime}_{0}]
,
and for all
a\in L
and
1\leq i<n
.
Furthermore, we will say that
\star
is
monotonic in
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}

and write
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
if it is
n
-monotonic in
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Clearly, if
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox^{n}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
then
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox^{m}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
for all
m\leq n
.
The next result shows that if
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
then we are guaranteed to avoid parallel timing anomalies.

C.6.8
.

If
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
as well as
U\preceq V
and
W\preceq W^{\prime}
,
then we have
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},x)
be an arbitrary time-bounded cylinder,
and let
\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}
be an arbitrary scheduler for
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W^{\prime}
.
Because
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
there exists a scheduler
\sigma_{V}
for
V
and a path
\pi
such that

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}}(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w^{\prime}_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t))

\displaystyle=\tau^{\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}}(\pi[1],a_{1})(\pi[2])\cdots\tau^{\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}}(\pi[n],a_{n})(\pi[n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho(\pi[1])*\dots*\rho(\pi[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{V}^{\sigma_{V}}(\pi_{V}[1],a_{1})(\pi_{V}[2])\cdots\tau_{V}^{\sigma_{V}}(\pi_{V}[n],a_{n})(\pi[n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho_{V}(\pi_{V}[1])*\dots*\rho_{V}(\pi_{V}[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{n+1}[v_{0}]}\tau_{V}^{\sigma_{V}}(\pi[1],a_{1})(\pi[2])\cdots\tau_{V}^{\sigma_{V}}(\pi[n],a_{n})(\pi[n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho_{V}(\pi[1])*\dots*\rho_{V}(\pi[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{V}}(v_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t)),

Since
U\preceq V
, there must exist some scheduler
\sigma_{U}
for
U
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{V}}(v_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t)))\leq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{U}}(u_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t)).

Again, since
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
there exists a scheduler
\sigma_{U,W}
for
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
such that

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{U}}(u_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{n+1}[u_{0}]}\tau_{U}^{\sigma_{U}}(\pi[1],a_{1})(\pi[2])\cdots\tau_{U}^{\sigma_{U}}(\pi[n],a_{n})(\pi[n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho_{U}(\pi[1])*\dots*\rho_{U}(\pi[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi_{W}\in\Pi_{n+1}[w_{0}]}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{n+1}[u_{0}]}\tau_{U}^{\sigma_{U}}(\pi[1],a_{1})(\pi[2])\cdots\tau_{U}^{\sigma_{U}}(\pi[n],a_{n})(\pi[n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho_{U}(\pi[1])*\dots*\rho_{U}(\pi[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{n+1}[u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0}]}\tau^{\sigma_{U,W}}(\pi[1],a_{1})([2])\cdots\tau^{\sigma_{U,W}}([n],a_{n})([n+1])

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\cdot(\rho(\pi[1])*\dots*\rho(\pi[n]))([0,t])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{U,W}}(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})(\mathfrak{C}(a_{1}\dots a_{n},t)).\qed

The special case where
W=W^{\prime}
shows that this
condition is sufficient to avoid parallel timing anomalies.
We do not know if it is decidable whether
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.
However, there is a stronger condition which is decidable
in the case of finite SMDPs.
We present it in the next definition.

C.6.9
.

We say that
\star
is
strongly

n
-monotonic in
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}

and write
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{n}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})

if
W^{\prime}
has a deterministic Markov kernel and for all state paths
\pi_{U}\in\Pi_{n}[u_{0}]
,

\pi_{V}\in\Pi_{n}[v_{0}]
,
\pi_{W}\in\Pi_{n}[w_{0}]
, and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{n}[w^{\prime}_{0}]
,
the first condition of Definition
C.6.7
is satisfied and

•

for all schedulers
\sigma_{U}
for
U
and all
schedulers
\sigma_{U,W}
for
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W
, it is the case that

\tau^{\sigma_{U,W}}(\pi_{U}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i],a)(\pi_{U}[i+1]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i+1])\geq\tau^{\sigma_{U}}_{U}(\pi_{U}[i],a)(\pi_{U}[i+1]),

and

•

for all schedulers
\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}
for
V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W^{\prime}

and all schedulers
\sigma_{V}
for
V
, it is the case that

\tau^{\sigma_{V}}_{V}(\pi_{V}[i],a)(\pi_{V}[i+1])\geq\tau^{\sigma_{V,W^{\prime}}}(\pi_{V}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i],a)(\pi_{V}[i+1]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i+1])

for all
a\in L
and
1\leq i<n
.
If
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{n}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we say that
\star
is
strongly
monotonic in
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}

and write
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.

The conditions of Definition
C.6.9
are
the second and third conditions from Definition
C.6.7
with
the existential quantifier strengthened to a universal quantifier.
It is obvious that
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
implies
(U,W)\mathbin{\lessapprox_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
,
and hence we get the following corollary.

C.6.10
.

If
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
as well as
U\preceq V
and
W\preceq W^{\prime}
,
then
U\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W\preceq V\mathbin{\|_{\star}}W^{\prime}
.

C.6.11
.

Let
U
and
V
be given by Figure
C.4.1

with
F_{\mu}\geq F_{\nu}
as in Example
C.4.9
.
Let
\star
be minimum rate composition and consider the context
W
from Figure
C.6.1
,
where
\mu^{\prime}=\mu
,
\nu^{\prime}=\nu
, and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
.
There is only one possible scheduler
\sigma
, which is the Dirac measure at
a
,
and hence it is clear that the second and third conditions are satisfied. We also find that

\displaystyle F_{\rho(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})}(t)=F_{\rho_{U}(u_{0})}(t)

\displaystyle F_{\rho_{V}(v_{0})}(t)=F_{\rho(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})}(t)

\displaystyle F_{\rho(u_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{1})}(t)=F_{\rho_{U}(u_{1})}(t)

\displaystyle F_{\rho_{V}(v_{1})}(t)=F_{\rho(v_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{1})}(t)

\displaystyle F_{\rho(u_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{2})}(t)=F_{\rho_{U}(u_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle F_{\rho_{V}(v_{2})}(t)=F_{\rho(v_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{2})}(t)

and hence the first condition is also satisfied, so
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W)
.

C.6.12
.

All the examples we gave in Section
C.6.1
are not monotonic,
and hence also not strongly monotonic, since they all violate condition 1 of monotonicity.

In Example
C.6.3
, this is because

F_{\rho(v_{0})}(t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[0.5\right](t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[5\right](t)=F_{\rho(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})}(t)

for any
t>0
. Likewise, in Example
C.6.4
we have

F_{\rho(u_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})}(t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[1\right](t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right](t)=F_{\rho(u_{0})}(t)

for any
t>0
. Finally, in Example
C.6.5
we have

F_{\rho(v_{0})}(t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[0.5\right](t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right](t)=F_{\rho(v_{0}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}w_{0})}(t)

for any
t>0
.

We now wish to show that it is decidable whether
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
for finite SMDPs,
thereby giving a decidable condition for avoiding timing anomalies.
To do this, we first show that in order to establish strong monotonicity,
it is enough to consider paths up to length

m=\max\{|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{W}|,|S_{V}|\cdot|S_{W^{\prime}}|\}+\max\{|S_{U}|,|S_{V}|,|S_{W}|,|S_{W^{\prime}}|\}+1,

due to the fact that they start repeating.

C.6.13
.

Let
U
and
V
be two finite, pointed SMDPs.
For any state paths
\pi_{U}
and
\pi_{V}

of length
l>|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
,
there will be
i<j\leq|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|+1
such that

\pi_{U}[i]=\pi_{U}[j]
,
\pi_{V}[i]=\pi_{V}[j]
.

Proof.

Since there are
|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
ways of choosing a pair
(u_{i},v_{j})\in S_{U}\times S_{V}

of states from
U
and
V
, if we pair the states of
\pi_{U}
and
\pi_{V}

such that we get the pairs
(\pi_{U}[1],\pi_{V}[1])
,
(\pi_{U}[2],\pi_{V}[2])
,
\dots
,
(\pi_{U}[l],\pi_{V}[l])
,
there must be two of these pairs that are the same because
l>|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
.
Hence we get states
\pi_{U}[i]=\pi_{U}[j]

and
\pi_{V}[i]=\pi_{V}[j]
with
i<j\leq n
.
It also follows that
i
and
j
can be chosen so that
i<j\leq|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
,
because otherwise we would have
j-i>|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
different pairs

(\pi_{U}[i],\pi_{V}[i]),(\pi_{U}[i+1],\pi_{V}[i+1]),\dots,(\pi_{U}[j],\pi_{V}[j]),

contradicting the fact that there are only
|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{V}|
such different pairs.
∎

C.6.14
.

Let
(U,u_{0})
,
(V,v_{0})
,
(W,w_{0})
, and
(W^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0})
be finite, pointed SMDPs.
If
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{m}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
, then
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Assume that
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{m}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.
Then
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{k}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
for all
k\leq m
.
Hence it remains to show that
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{k}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
for all
k>m
.

Let
k>m
and consider two state paths
\pi_{U}=u_{1}u_{2}\dots u_{k}

and
\pi_{W}=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{k}
of
U
and
W
, respectively, both of length
k
.
By Lemma
C.6.13
there must exist
i<j\leq|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{W}|+1
such that

u_{i}=u_{j}
and
w_{i}=w_{j}
.
Since there exists a state path from
u_{1}
to
u_{i}
,
it must be possible to reach this state in less than
|S_{U}|
steps,
and likewise for
W
. Hence there must exist
l\leq\max\{|S_{U}|,|S_{W}|\}
,
and state paths

u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}\dots u_{l}^{\prime}u_{i}u_{i+1}\dots u_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{1}w_{2}^{\prime}\dots w_{l}^{\prime}w_{i}w_{i+1}\dots w_{j}

of length

l+(j-i)\leq\max\{|S_{U}|,|S_{W}|\}+|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{W}|+1\leq m
.
Hence we know that the conditions of Definition
C.6.9

are satisfied for
u_{i}\dots u_{j}
and
w_{i}\dots w_{j}
.
By removing the states
u_{i+1}\dots u_{j}
and
w_{i+1}\dots w_{j}

from
\pi_{U}
and
\pi_{W}
we end up with two new state paths
\pi^{\prime}_{U}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{W}

of length
k^{\prime}=k-(j-i)
.
We can keep doing this as long as
k^{\prime}>m
,
so at some point we must end up with state paths
\pi^{*}_{U}
and
\pi^{*}_{W}

of length
k^{*}\leq m
,
for which the conditions of Definition
C.6.9
are satisfied by assumption,
and hence they are satisfied for all of
\pi_{U}
and
\pi_{W}
.
The same argument can be applied to two state paths
\pi_{V}
and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}

of
V
and
W^{\prime}
, so we conclude that
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.
∎

We can now use the first-order theory of the reals to show
that strong monotonicity is a decidable property.

C.6.15
.

Consider the finite pointed SMDPs
(U,u_{0})
,
(V,v_{0})
,
(W,w_{0})
, and
(W^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0})
.
If for all state paths
\pi_{U}\in\Pi_{m}[u_{0}]
,

\pi_{V}\in\Pi_{m}[v_{0}]
,
\pi_{W}\in\Pi_{m}[w_{0}]
, and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{m}[w^{\prime}_{0}]

we have that
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mid F_{\rho(\pi_{U}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho_{U}(\pi_{U}[i])}(t)\}

and
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mid F_{\rho_{V}(\pi_{V}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho(\pi_{V}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i])}(t)\}

are semialgebraic sets for all
1\leq i\leq m
, then it is decidable whether
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Note first of all that since
L
and
W^{\prime}
are finite,
it is decidable whether
W^{\prime}
has a deterministic Markov kernel by looking at all the states.
By Lemma
C.6.14
, it suffices to check
whether
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq^{m}_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
where

m=\max\{|S_{U}|\cdot|S_{W}|,|S_{V}|\cdot|S_{W^{\prime}}|\}+\max\{|S_{U}|,|S_{V}|,|S_{W}|,|S_{W^{\prime}}|\}+1.

This can be done by exploiting the decidability
of the first-order theory of the reals in the following way.
Since
L
is finite and
U
,
V
,
W
, and
W^{\prime}
are all finite,
there are finitely many state paths
\pi_{U}\in\Pi_{m}[u_{0}]
,

\pi_{V}\in\Pi_{m}[v_{0}]
,
\pi_{W}\in\Pi_{m}[w_{0}]
, and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{m}[w^{\prime}_{0}]
.
Because of this, and since the sets

\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mid F_{\rho(\pi_{U}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho_{U}(\pi_{U}[i])}(t)\}

and

\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mid F_{\rho_{V}(\pi_{V}[i])}(t)\geq F_{\rho(\pi_{V}[i]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W^{\prime}}[i])}(t)\},

which we need to check for the first condition,
were assumed to be semialgebraic,
it is possible to express the conditions of Definition
C.6.9

in the first-order theory of the reals,
using finitely many quantifiers and inequalities.
Since the first-order theory of the reals is decidable,
the truth value of the resulting formula is decidable.
∎

For uniform and exponential distributions with minimum or maximum composition,
the corresponding sets are all semialgebraic,
and the same is true for exponential distributions with product composition.
Theorem
C.6.15
can therefore be used for these types of composition.

Unfortunately, strong monotonicity is a very strict requirement.
In effect, it requires that there is only one possible action,
and hence rules out non-determinism.
However, strong monotonicity still makes sense as a requirement
on processes with no non-determinism,
since all our examples of timing anomalies in Section
C.6.1

are of this form.

C.6.16
.

If
(U,W)\mathbin{\leqq_{\star}}(V,W^{\prime})
, then
L
is a singleton set or
u_{0}
is a deadlock state.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive.
Suppose
|L|>1
and
u_{0}
is not a deadlock state.
Because
u_{0}
is not a deadlock state,
there must exist some state path
\pi_{U}
such that

\pi_{U}[1]=u_{0}
and
\tau_{U}(\pi_{U}[1],a)(\pi_{U}[2])>0
for some
a\in L
.
Since
|L|>1
, we can find some
b\in L
with
a\neq b
.
Now construct schedulers given by
\sigma_{U,W}(s)=\delta_{b}
and
\sigma_{U}(s)=\delta_{a}

for any state
s
.
Then

\tau^{\sigma_{U,W}}(\pi_{U}[1]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\pi_{W}[1],a)(\tau_{U}[2]\mathbin{\|_{\star}}\tau_{W}[2])=0

but

\tau_{U}^{\sigma_{U}}(\pi_{U}[1],a)(\tau_{U}[2])>0,

and hence the first condition of Definition
C.6.9

is violated.
∎

Conclusion

In this paper, we have investigated the notion of a process being faster than another process in the context of semi-Markov decision processes.
We have given a trace-based definition of a faster-than relation,
and shown that this definition is closely connected to convolutions of distributions.
The faster-than relation is unfortunately undecidable,
but we have shown how to approximate a time-bounded version of it.
By considering composition as being parametric in how the residence times of states are combined,
we have given examples showing that our faster-than relation gives rise to parallel timing anomalies
for many of the popular ways of composing rates.
We have therefore given sufficient conditions for how such parallel timing anomalies can be avoided,
and we have shown that these conditions are decidable.

The main challenge that we face when trying to construct algorithms
for the faster-than relation is that of schedulers,
and in particular the juxtaposition between the universal and existential
quantification over schedulers.
For example, we had to strengthen the existential quantifier
to a universal one in order to decide the conditions for avoiding parallel timing anomalies.
This is because we know that locally, for any scheduler
\sigma
,
there exists a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}
which works.
However, it is not clear that all of these
\sigma^{\prime}

can be collected coherently into a single scheduler which works globally.
Solving this challenge would allow us to
decide the property of monotonicity instead of the too-strong property of strong monotonicity,
as well as prove decidability for so-called unambiguous processes.

The conditions we have given for avoiding timing anomalies
do not look at the context in isolation,
but depend also on the processes that are being swapped.
It would be preferable to have conditions on a context
that would guarantee the absence of parallel timing anomalies
no matter what processes are being swapped.

Paper D
A Hemimetric Extension of Simulation for Semi-Markov Processes

Mathias R. Pedersen, Giorgio Bacci, Kim G. Larsen, and Radu Mardare

The paper has been published in the

Proceedings of the 15th International Conference on Quantitative Evaluation of Systems
, pp. 339–355, 2018.

© 2018 Springer

The layout has been revised and the content extended.

Introduction

Semi-Markov decision processes (SMDPs) are Markovian stochastic decision processes modelling
the firing time of transitions via real-valued random variables describing the residence-time on states.
Semi-Markov decision processes provide a more permissive model than continuous-time Markov decision processes,
since they allow as residence-time distributions any generic distribution on the positive real line,
rather than only exponential ones.
The generality offered by SMDPs has been found useful in modelling several real-case scenarios.
Successful examples include power plants
[
PST97
]
and power supply units
[
PTV04
]
, to name a few.

When considering such real-time stochastic processes,
non-functional requirements are important,
particularly requirements like response time and throughput,
which depend on the timing behaviour of the process.
We therefore wish to understand and be able to compare
the timing behaviour of different processes.

To cope with the need for comparing the timing behaviour of different systems,
in this paper we propose and study a quantitative extension of the simulation relation
by Baier et al.
[
baier2000
]
,
called
\varepsilon
-simulation, which puts the focus on the timing aspect of processes.
The intuition is that a process
s_{2}

\varepsilon
-simulates

another process
s_{1}

if after accelerating the actions of
s_{2}
by a factor
\varepsilon>0

it reacts to the inputs from the external environment as
s_{1}
with at least the same speed.

This type of quantitative reasoning is not new in the literature,
and it dates back to the seminal work
of Jou and Smolka
[
jou1990
,
GJS90
]
,
who proposed the concept of probabilistic
\varepsilon
-bisimulation.
This line of work has lead to much work on probabilistic bisimulation distances
[
CBW12
,
DGJP04
,
FPP11
]
.
While our work is conceptually similar to the bisimulation distances,
it is technically very different.
This is because bisimulation distances are constructed from a coalgebraic view as fixed points of operators.
However, for the kind of timed systems that we are investigating,
the coalgebraic perspective is much less understood.
Moreover, since our distance generalises a preorder relation and not a congruence
as the other distances do, it is not symmetric,
which brings in new technical challenges

Following the work of Jou and Smolka,
our notion of
\varepsilon
-simulation naturally induces a distance between processes:
For any pair of states
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, we define their
simulation distance

as the least acceleration factor needed by
s_{2}
to speed up its actions
in order to behave at least as fast as
s_{1}
. This definition does not provide a distance
in the usual sense, but rather a
multiplicative hemimetric
,
i.e. an asymmetric notion of distance satisfying
a multiplicative version of the triangle inequality.
Such a notion is not new, as it is extensively applied in the context of differential privacy
to measure information leakage of systems (see e.g.
[
ACMMPS14
,
CABP13
]
).

The theoretical relevance and applicability of the simulation distance
is argued by means of the following results,
which are the main technical contributions of the paper:

1.

We provide an algorithm for computing the simulation distance between arbitrary states
of an SMDP running in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n)
, where
n
is the
number of states,
m
the number of actions,
k
the number of
atomic propositions, and
f(l)
the complexity of comparing the residence time distributions
on states.

2.

We show that under some mild conditions on how
residence-time distributions are combined in the
parallel composition of two states, CSP-like parallel
composition of SMDPs is non-expansive with respect to our hemimetric.
This shows that the simulation distance is suitable for compositional reasoning.

3.

We provide a logical characterisation of the distance
in terms of a simple Markovian logic,
stating that the distance from
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
is less than or equal to
\varepsilon
if and only if

s_{2}
satisfies the
\varepsilon
-perturbation of any logical property that
s_{1}
satisfies.
Moreover, we prove that
\varepsilon
-simulation preserves the
\varepsilon
-perturbation
of time-bounded reachability properties.

4.

We show that sets of formulas in our logic are closed in the topology induced by the distance.
This means that approximate reasoning is sound in the limit:
If a sequence of state converging to a limit all satisfy some logical property,
then the limit will also satisfy this property.

Notation and Preliminaries.

Let
\mathbb{N}
denote the natural numbers,

\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
the non-negative rational numbers,

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the non-negative real numbers,
and
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the strictly positive ones.
We equip the real numbers with the usual Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Given a set
X
, we will denote by
\mathcal{D}(X)

the set of all probability measures on
X
.
A probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(X)
is said to be
finitely supported
if the set

\{x\in X\mid\mu(x)>0\}
is finite.
If
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
,
then the
cumulative distribution function
(CDF)
will be denoted by
F_{\mu}
and is given by
F_{\mu}(t)=\mu([0,t])
.
Any CDF
F
is increasing, meaning that if
t\geq t^{\prime}
, then
F(t)\geq F(t^{\prime})
,
and also right-continuous,
meaning that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0

such that if
t<t^{\prime}<t+\delta
, then
|F(t^{\prime})-F(t)|<\varepsilon
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we will write
\delta_{x}
for the Dirac measure at
x
,
which is defined by
\delta_{x}(E)=1
if
x\in E
and
\delta_{x}(E)=0
otherwise.
For any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
we will write
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
for the CDF of an exponential distribution with rate
\theta
,
and for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
a<b
,
we will write
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
for the CDF of a uniform distribution.

We will use the convention that
\infty+x=\infty
for
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\infty\cdot y=\infty
for
y\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
A function
d\colon X\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
is called a

hemimetric
if it satisfies
d(x,x)=0
and the triangle inequality
d(x,z)\leq d(x,y)+d(y,z)
.
It is called a
pseudometric
if it is also symmetric, i.e.
d(x,y)=d(y,x)
,
and it is called a
metric
if it is symmetric and furthermore
d(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
.

Semi-Markov Decision Processes

In this section, we introduce semi-Markov decision processes,
which are continuous-time reactive probabilistic systems.
A semi-Markov decision process has residence time on states governed
by generic distributions on the positive real line and reacts to inputs from an external environment by making a probabilistic transition to a next state.

Hereafter, we consider a non-empty finite set of
input actions

A
,
and a non-empty, finite set of
atomic propositions

\mathcal{AP}
.

D.2.1
.

A
semi-Markov decision process
(SMDP) is given by a tuple
M\penalty\ =\penalty\ (S,\tau,\rho,L)
where

•

S
is a non-empty, countable set of
states
,

•

\tau:S\times A\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(S)
is the
transition function
,

•

\rho:S\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
is the
residence-time function
, and

•

L:S\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{AP}}
is the
labelling function
.

The operational behaviour of an SMDP is as follows. The SMDP at a given state
s\in S
,
after receiving an input
a\in A
, goes to state
s^{\prime}\in S
within time
t

with probability
\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\rho(s)([0,t])
.
An SMDP is said to be
finite
if it has a finite set of states,
and it is said to be
finitely supported
if its transition function
\tau(s,a)

is finitely supported for every
s
and
a
.
For
s\in S
, we will write
F_{s}
for the CDF of
\rho(s)
, i.e.
F_{s}(t)=\rho(s)([0,t])
.

Continuous-time Markov decision processes are a special case of SMDPs in which
all residence-time functions are exponentially distributed,
whereas discrete-time Markov decision processes are a special case of SMDPs where
the residence-time distribution in each state is the Dirac measure at
0
,
representing the fact that transitions are taken instantaneously.

In defining simulation and bisimulation for SMDPs,
we will use ingredients from the definition of simulation
and bisimulation for Markov decision processes
[
segala1995
]

and simulation and bisimulation for continuous-time Markov chains
[
BKHW05
]
.
However, since we are also generalising to arbitrary distributions on time
rather than just exponential distributions,
the condition on rates for exponential distributions must be replaced with
a more general condition on the distributions.
There is a rich literature on so-called stochastic orders
[
shaked2007
]
,
which impose an ordering on random variables.
We will consider here the most commonly used of these,
known as the
usual stochastic order
.

D.2.2
.

For an SMDP
M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
,
a relation
R\subseteq S\times S
is a
simulation

(resp.
bisimulation
) on
M
if for all
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
we have

1.

L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
,

2.

F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
(resp.
F_{s_{2}}(t)=F_{s_{1}}(t)
) for all

t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and

3.

for all
a\in A
there exists a
weight function
or
coupling

\Delta_{a}\colon S\times S\rightarrow[0,1]
between
\tau(s_{1},a)
and
\tau(s_{2},a)
such that

(a)

\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})>0
implies
(s,s^{\prime})\in R
,

(b)

\tau(s_{1},a)(s)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})
for all
s\in S
, and

(c)

\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})
for all
s^{\prime}\in S
.

We say that
s_{2}

simulates
(resp. is
bisimilar to
)
s_{1}
, written

s_{1}\penalty\ \precsim\penalty\ s_{2}
(resp.
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
), if there is a simulation (resp. bisimulation) relation
containing
(s_{1},s_{2})
.

It is easy to show that the
similarity
relation
\precsim
is the largest simulation relation,
and analogously that the
bisimilarity
relation
\sim
is the largest bisimulation relation.
The coupling ensures that the simulation relation is preserved by successor states.
Intuitively,
s_{1}
simulates
s_{2}
if the CDF of
\rho(s_{2})
is pointwise
greater than or equal to the CDF of
\rho(s_{1})
, and the transition probability distribution
of
s_{1}
can be matched by the transition probability function
s_{2}
by means of a coupling,
in such a way that if two successor states
s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{2}^{\prime}
have a non-zero coupling,
then
s_{1}^{\prime}
again simulates
s_{2}^{\prime}
. For bisimulation, we instead require that the CDFs
behave exactly the same in each step.

Given a set
C\subseteq S
and a relation
R\subseteq S\times S
, let

R(C)=\{s^{\prime}\in S\mid(s,s^{\prime})\in R\text{ for some }s\in C\}

be the
R
-closure of
C
.
If
R
is a preorder,
R(C)
is the upward closure of
C
.

The following result, which is a trivial generalisation of
[
SZ12
, Lemma 1]
,
gives a different but equivalent definition of simulation which is sometimes useful.

D.2.3
.

R\subseteq S\times S
is a simulation relation if and only if
for any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
we have

1.

L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
,

2.

F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and

3.

\tau(s_{1},a)(C)\leq\tau(s_{2},a)(R(C))
for all
C\subseteq S
.

The following generalises
[
BKHW05
, Proposition 25(3)]
to the case of SMDPs.

D.2.4
.

{\precsim}\cap{\precsim^{-1}}={\sim}
.

Proof.

First assume that
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
.
Then
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
.
Also,
F_{s_{2}}(t)=F_{s_{2}}(t)
for all
t
,
so clearly
F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
and
F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq F_{s_{2}}(t)
for all
t
.
For any subset
C\subseteq S
we have

\tau(s_{1},a)(C)\leq\tau(s_{1},a)(\mathord{\sim}(C))=\tau(s_{2},a)(\mathord{\sim}(C))

and

\tau(s_{2},a)(C)\leq\tau(s_{2},a)(\mathord{\sim}(C))=\tau(s_{1},a)(\mathord{\sim}(C))\kern 5.0pt,

so
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
and
s_{1}\precsim^{-1}s_{2}
.

Now assume that
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
and
s_{1}\precsim^{-1}s_{2}
.
Clearly
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
.
Since
F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
and
F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq F_{s_{2}}(t)
for all
t
,
it follows that
F_{s_{2}}(t)=F_{s_{1}}(t)
for all
t
.
Now take an arbitrary subset
C\subseteq S

and let
B=\mathord{\precsim\cap\precsim^{-1}}(C)
,
C_{1}=\mathord{\precsim}(B)
,
and
C_{2}=C_{1}\setminus B
. Then

\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{1})=\tau(s_{1},a)(\mathord{\precsim}(C_{1}))\leq\tau(s_{2},a)(\mathord{\precsim}(C_{1}))=\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{1})

and

\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{1})=\tau(s_{2},a)(\mathord{\precsim}(C_{1}))\leq\tau(s_{1},a)(\mathord{\precsim}(C_{1}))=\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{1})\kern 5.0pt,

so
\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{1})=\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{1})
,
and likewise we can show that
\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{2})=\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{2})
.
Since we can write

\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{1})=\tau(s_{1},a)(B)+\tau(s_{1},a)(C_{2})

and

\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{1})=\tau(s_{2},a)(B)+\tau(s_{2},a)(C_{2})\kern 5.0pt,

this together implies that
\tau(s_{1},a)(B)=\tau(s_{2},a)(B)
.
Hence we conclude that
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
.
∎

The above is analogous to a result stating that bisimulation and simulation equivalence
coincide for deterministic labelled transition systems
[
baier2000
]
.
In our case, Proposition
D.2.4
holds because
reactive systems are inherently deterministic.

Comparing the Speed of Residence-Time Distributions

For comparing the random variables describing the residence time on states,
the similarity relation uses the usual stochastic order:
if
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
then, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

F_{s_{1}}(t)\leq F_{s_{2}}(t)
.
In words, if
s_{2}
simulates
s_{1}
,
it is more likely that
s_{2}
will take a transition before
s_{1}
,
that is,
s_{2}
is stochastically faster than
s_{1}
in reacting to an input.

In this section, we propose a different way of comparing residence-time distributions.
The idea is to get quantitative information about how much a distribution should
be accelerated to become at least as fast as another distribution.

D.3.1
.

Let
F
and
G
be CDFs and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
We say that
F
is
\varepsilon
-faster than

G
,
written
F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G
,
if for all
t
we have
F(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq G(t)
.

Consider two states
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
,
having residence time governed by the distributions
F_{s_{1}}
and
F_{s_{2}}
, respectively,
and assume that
F_{s_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{s_{2}}
holds.
If
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
, then this means that

s_{1}
is stochastically faster than
s_{2}
, even if the residence time in

s_{1}
is slowed down by a factor
\varepsilon
.
If instead we have
\varepsilon>1
,
then
s_{1}
is stochastically slower than
s_{2}
,
but if we accelerate its residence-time distribution by a factor
\varepsilon
,
then it becomes stochastically faster than
s_{2}
.

In the rest of the section we will argue that
\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}

is a good notion for gathering quantitative
information about the speed of residence-time distributions on states.
We will do this by comparing the most common distributions used in the literature
for modelling residence time on states on stochastic systems:
Dirac distributions, exponential distributions, and uniform distributions.

The Dirac measure at zero is the fastest measure,
in the following sense.

D.3.2
.

Let
F
be any CDF. The following holds for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

1.

\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F
.

2.

If
F\neq\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
, then
F\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
.

Proof.

The first point is clear, since
\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right](t)=1\geq F(t)
for any
t
.

For the second point, note that
\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
is the only CDF such that

\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right](0)=1,

and hence
F(\varepsilon\cdot 0)<\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right](0)
for any
\varepsilon\geq 1
.
∎

For comparing exponential distributions,
it is simple to show that it is enough to accelerate by the ratio between the two rates.
The same is true for uniform distributions,
except we also need to consider whether the uniform distributions start at
0
,
since if a uniform distribution starts at
0
,
then we can only hope to make another uniform distribution faster than it
if this other uniform distribution also starts at
0
.
To prove this, we make use of the following two lemmas.

D.3.3
.

For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
it holds that

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\varepsilon\cdot\theta\right](t)
.

Proof.

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=1-e^{-\theta\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot t)}=1-e^{-(\theta\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot t}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\varepsilon\cdot\theta\right](t).\qed

D.3.4
.

For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
it holds that

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a}{\varepsilon},\frac{b}{\varepsilon}\right](t)
.

Proof.

\frac{\varepsilon\cdot t-a}{b-a}=0\implies\varepsilon\cdot t-a=0\implies t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon}

and

\frac{\varepsilon\cdot t-a}{b-a}=1\implies\varepsilon\cdot t-a=b-a\implies t=\frac{b}{\varepsilon}.

Hence

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a}{\varepsilon},\frac{b}{\varepsilon}\right](t)
.
∎

D.3.5
.

1.

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
,
where
\varepsilon=\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}
.

2.

If
c=0
and
a>0
, then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]

for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

3.

If
c=0
and
a=0
, then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
,
where
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d}
.

4.

If
c>0
, then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
,
where
\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\right\}
.

In all cases, the given
\varepsilon
is the least such that
the
\varepsilon
-faster than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

We see that

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=1-e^{-\theta_{1}\varepsilon\cdot t}=1-e^{-\theta_{2}t}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t).

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
, then take some
t>0
to get

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=1-e^{-\theta_{1}\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t}<1-e^{-\theta_{1}\varepsilon\cdot t}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t),

and hence
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
.

2.

Let
c=0
and
a>0
.
Take an arbitrary
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

and let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon}>0
in order to get

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0
.
However,
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)>0
for any
t>0
,
so
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
.

3.

Let
c=0
and
a=0
,
and take
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d}
.
Then

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{d}{b},d\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[0,d\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

To show that it is the least
\varepsilon
such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than
relation holds, let
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{b}{d}
.
Then

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d)<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\left(\frac{b}{d}\cdot d\right)=1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d).

4.

Now let
c>0
and
\varepsilon=\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}
.
If
\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}=\frac{a}{c}
,
then

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,b\cdot\frac{c}{a}\right](t).

Since
\frac{a}{c}\geq\frac{b}{d}
we get
\frac{c}{a}\leq\frac{d}{b}
,
and hence

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,b\cdot\frac{c}{a}\right](t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,b\cdot\frac{d}{b}\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

On the other hand, if
\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}=\frac{b}{d}
,
then we get
\frac{c}{a}\geq\frac{d}{b}
, and hence

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{d}{b},d\right](t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

It remains to prove that this is the least
\varepsilon
such that this relation holds.
Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}
.
If
\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}=\frac{a}{c}
,
then let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}>\frac{a}{\frac{a}{c}}=c
.
Then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0
,
but
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)>0
since
t>c
.
On the other hand, if
\max\{\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{d}\}=\frac{b}{d}
,
then

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d)<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\left(\frac{b}{d}\cdot d\right)=1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d).\qed

Moreover, an exponential distribution can never be made faster than a uniform distribution,
since uniform distributions become
1
eventually, but exponential distributions
tend asymptotically to
1
but never reach it.
Furthermore, whether or not a uniform distribution
can be made faster than an exponential distribution depends on its value at
0
.

D.3.6
.

1.

\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

2.

If
a>0
, then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

3.

If
a=0
, then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
,
where
\varepsilon=\theta\cdot b
.
Furthermore, this is the least
\varepsilon
such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

We have
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](b)=1
, but
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)<1
for all
t
,
and hence
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]

for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

2.

If
a>0
, then let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be given,
and let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon}
.
Then
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0
,
but
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)>0
since
t>0
,
and therefore
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
.

3.

If
a=0
, then let
\varepsilon=\theta\cdot b
.
Clearly
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot 0)=0
and
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](0)=0
.
We see that
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\frac{\theta bt}{b}

and
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)=1-e^{-\theta t}
have the same derivative from the right at
0
,
namely
\theta
. Hence the slope of these two functions is the same in
0
,
but since the slope of an exponential distribution is always decreasing,
this means that
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\theta\cdot b
,
then the slope in
0
of
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)

must be less than that of
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
,
so there will exist some
t>0
such that

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)
,
and hence
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
. ∎

The
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation enjoys a kind of monotonicity property,
which is simply a consequence of the fact that CDFs are increasing.

D.3.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
and assume that
F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G
.
Then
F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}G
.

Proof.

F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G
means that
F(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq G(t)
for all
t
.
Since
F
is non-decreasing and
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
,
this means that
F(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)\geq F(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq G(t)
for all
t
,
so
F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}G
.
∎

The probability distribution of the sum of two independent random variables is the
convolution

of their individual distributions. The general formula for the convolution of two measures

\mu
and
\nu
on the real line is given by

(\mu*\nu)(E)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(E-x)\;\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x}).

Notably, the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation is a congruence
with respect to convolution of measures.

D.3.8
.

If
F_{\mu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu_{2}}
and
F_{\nu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{2}}
,
then
F_{(\mu_{1}*\nu_{1})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{(\mu_{2}*\nu_{2})}
.

Proof.

Define the transformation
T(x)=\varepsilon\cdot x

and let
\nu_{1}^{\prime}([0,t])=\nu_{1}\left(\left[0,\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right]\right)
.
Then we see that

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(T^{-1}([0,t]))

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}(\{x\mid x\cdot\varepsilon\in[0,t]\})

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\left(\left\{x\mid x\in\left[0,t/\varepsilon\right]\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\left(\left[0,\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}^{\prime}([0,t]).

Because
F_{\mu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu_{2}}

we know that
\mu_{1}([0,\varepsilon\cdot t])\geq\mu_{2}([0,t])
for all
t
,
and since
F_{\nu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{2}}
,
we know that
\nu_{1}([0,\varepsilon\cdot t)=\nu_{1}^{\prime}([0,t])\geq\nu_{2}([0,t])

for all
t
.
We can therefore do following series of transformations
[
panangaden2009
, Proposition 3.8]
.

\displaystyle(\mu_{1}*\nu_{1})([0,\varepsilon\cdot t])

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{1}([0,\varepsilon\cdot t-x])\;\nu_{1}(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{1}([0,\varepsilon\cdot t-T(x)])\;\nu_{1}^{\prime}(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{1}([0,\varepsilon(t-x))\;\nu_{1}^{\prime}(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{2}([0,t-x])\;\nu_{1}^{\prime}(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{2}([0,t-x])\;\nu_{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{d}x})

\displaystyle=(\mu_{2}*\nu_{2})([0,t]).\qed

In Section
D.7.1
we will see that the above property
is essential for the preservation of reachability properties.
Intuitively, this is because convolution corresponds to
sequential composition of the residence-time behaviour.

There are other possible ways to compare the relative speed
of residence-time distributions quantitatively.
In the following we explore some alternative definitions
of the notion of the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation,
and argue that none of them are preferable to the one given in Definition
D.3.1
.
Given two CDFs
F
and
G
, we consider the following three alternative definitions of

F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G
:

1.

for all
t
,
F(t)\cdot\varepsilon\geq G(t)
,

2.

for all
t
,
F(t)+\varepsilon\geq G(t)
, and

3.

for all
t
,
F(\varepsilon+t)\geq G(t)
.

If
\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
1
), then we see that
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
, for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
whenever
c<a
. This is because

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)\cdot\varepsilon=0<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](a)
. Hence we lose the properties of
Proposition
D.3.5
.

If
\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
2
), we trivially get that whenever

\varepsilon\geq 1
,
F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G
, for any two CDFs
F
and
G
.
Hence (2) is only interesting for
0\leq\varepsilon<1
.
However, even in this case we would still lose the properties of Proposition
D.3.5
.
Indeed, whenever
a\geq d
,
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
, for any
0\leq\varepsilon<1
. This follows because
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)+\varepsilon=\varepsilon<1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](a)
.

Lastly, if
\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
3
), then it would not be a congruence
with respect to convolution of distributions, i.e., Proposition
D.3.8
would not hold.
For a counterexample, take
F_{\mu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[2,4\right]
,
F_{\mu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[1,3\right]
,

F_{\nu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[3,4\right]
, and
F_{\nu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[2,4\right]
.
Then
F_{\mu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{1}F_{\mu_{2}}
and
F_{\nu_{1}}\sqsubseteq_{1}F_{\nu_{2}}
,
but
F_{(\mu_{1}*\mu_{2})}\not\sqsubseteq_{1}F_{(\nu_{1}*\nu_{2})}
.

A Hemimetric for Semi-Markov Decision Processes

In this section, we are going to extend the definition of simulation relation between SMDPs
to the quantitative setting.
We will see that this relation naturally induces a notion of distance between SMDPs,
describing the least acceleration factor required globally
on the residence-time distributions to make a given SMDP as fast as another one.

D.4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For an SMDP
M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
,
a relation
R\subseteq S\times S
is an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation

on
M
if for all
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
we have
that the first and third condition for simulation are satisfied,
and
F_{s_{2}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{s_{1}}
.
We say that
s_{2}

\varepsilon
-simulates
s_{1}
, written
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
if there is an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R
such that
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
.

D.4.2
.

Let
A=\{a\}
and consider the SMDP
M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
given by
S=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
,

\tau(s_{1},a)(s_{1})=1=\tau(s_{2},a)(s_{2})
,

F_{s_{1}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[4\right]
,
F_{s_{2}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[2\right]
, and
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
.
By Proposition
D.3.5
we see that
s_{1}\precsim_{2}s_{2}
and
s_{2}\precsim_{\frac{1}{2}}s_{1}
.

It is easy to show that the
\varepsilon
-similarity
relation
\precsim_{\varepsilon}
is the
largest simulation relation, and with the previous section in mind, one immediately sees that

\precsim_{1}
coincides with
\precsim
. Moreover, the following holds.

D.4.3
.

For any
\varepsilon\leq 1
, if
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
, then
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
R\subseteq S\times S
be an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
such that
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
.
We will now argue that
R
is also a simulation relation.
The first condition is clear.
For the second condition, we have

F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t).

The third condition is satisfied because
R
is an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation.
∎

If
\varepsilon>1
, the above implication does not hold. For an easy counterexample
consider
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
from Example
D.4.2
where
s_{1}\precsim_{2}s_{2}

but
s_{1}\not\precsim s_{2}
.

For
\varepsilon>1
, we can obtain a result similar to Proposition
D.4.3

only if we “accelerate” the overall behaviour of
s_{2}
. Formally, for a given SMDP

M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
, we define the SMDP

M_{\varepsilon}=(S_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon},\rho_{\varepsilon},L_{\varepsilon})
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{\varepsilon}&=S\cup\{(s)_{\varepsilon}\mid s\in S\},\\
L_{\varepsilon}(s)&=L(s),\\
L_{\varepsilon}((s)_{\varepsilon})&=L(s),\\
\rho_{\varepsilon}(s)([0,t])&=\rho(s)([0,t]),\\
\rho_{\varepsilon}((s)_{\varepsilon})([0,t])&=\rho(s)([0,\varepsilon\cdot t]),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tau_{\varepsilon}(s,a)(s^{\prime})&=\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime}),\\
\tau_{\varepsilon}(s,a)((s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon})&=0,\\
\tau_{\varepsilon}((s)_{\varepsilon},a)(s^{\prime})&=0,\\
\tau_{\varepsilon}((s)_{\varepsilon},a)((s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon})&=\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime}).\end{aligned}

Intuitively, the states
s\in S
in
M_{\varepsilon}
are identical copies of those in
M
,
whereas the states
(s)_{\varepsilon}
react to each input
a\in A
functionally
identically to
s
but faster, since the residence-time on the states
are all equally accelerated by a factor
\varepsilon
,
thus
(s)_{\varepsilon}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s
.
For this reason
(s)_{\varepsilon}
is called the
\varepsilon
-acceleration of
s
.

Given the definition of accelerated state, Proposition
D.4.3
can be
generalised to arbitrary values of
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
in the following way.

D.4.4
.

For any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
if and only if
s_{1}\precsim(s_{2})_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

(\implies)
Let
R\subseteq S\times S
be an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
with
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
. Define

R^{\prime}=\{(s,(s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}\times S_{\varepsilon}\mid(s,s^{\prime})\in R\},

and take an arbitrary
(s,(s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon})\in R^{\prime}
.
The first condition of Definition
D.4.1

is satisfied because
F_{(s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}}(t)=F_{s^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s}(t)
.

For the second condition, we know that for any
a\in A
there exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}
,
and we now define

\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s^{\prime\prime}\notin S\text{ or }s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S\\
\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})>0

\displaystyle\implies\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})>0

\displaystyle\implies(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})\in R

\displaystyle\implies(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})\in R^{\prime},

condition (a) is also satisfied.
For condition (b), first consider the case where
s^{\prime\prime}\in S
.
Then we get

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau_{\varepsilon}(s,a)(s^{\prime\prime}).

For the case where
s^{\prime\prime}\notin S
we get

\sum_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})=0

and

\tau_{\varepsilon}(s,a)(s^{\prime\prime})=0.

Likewise, for condition (c), first consider the case where
s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S
.
Then

\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})=0

and

\tau_{\varepsilon}((s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon},a)(s^{\prime\prime\prime})=0.

For the case where
s^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin S
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s^{\prime},a)(s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau_{\varepsilon}((s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon},a)((s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon}).

(\impliedby)
Let
R\subseteq S_{\varepsilon}\times S_{\varepsilon}

be a simulation relation with
(s_{1},(s_{2})_{\varepsilon})\in R

and define

R^{\prime}=\{(s,s^{\prime})\in S\times S\mid(s,(s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon})\in R\}.

For an arbitrary
(s,s^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}
we get

F_{s^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{(s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}}\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
,
thus satisfying the first condition.

We know that for any
a\in A
there exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}
,
and we now define

\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})=\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon}).

This coupling satisfies condition (a) because

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})>0

\displaystyle\implies\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})>0

\displaystyle\implies(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})\in R

\displaystyle\implies(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})\in R^{\prime}.

For condition (b), we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}}\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\tau_{\varepsilon}(s,a)(s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s,a)(s^{\prime\prime}).

Likewise, for condition (c) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s^{\prime\prime},(s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\tau_{\varepsilon}((s^{\prime})_{\varepsilon},a)((s^{\prime\prime\prime})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\tau(s^{\prime},a)(s^{\prime\prime\prime}).\qed

The relevance of the above statement is twofold:
it clarifies the relation between
\precsim_{\varepsilon}
and
\precsim
,
and also provides a way to modify the behaviour
of a state
s_{2}
of an SMDP in order to simulate
a state
s_{1}
whenever
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
holds.

Having this characterisation of similarity in terms of acceleration of processes one can think
about the following problem:
given two states,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
such that
s_{1}\not\precsim s_{2}
, what
is the least
\varepsilon\geq 1
(if it exists) such that
s_{1}\precsim(s_{2})_{\varepsilon}
holds?
We can answer this question by means of the following distance.

D.4.5
.

The
simulation distance

d\colon S\times S\to[1,\infty]

between two states
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
is given by

d(s_{1},s_{2})=\inf\{\varepsilon\geq 1\mid s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\}.

As usual, if there is no
\varepsilon\geq 1
such that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
, then

d(s_{1},s_{2})=\infty
, because
\inf\Emptyset=\infty
.
It is clear from the definition that
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
implies
d(s_{1},s_{2})=1
.
For finitely supported SMDPs, the converse is also true.
However, the proof of this makes use of the logical characterisation of the faster-than relation,
so we delay the proof until Section
D.7
where we discuss logical properties.

Note that the definition above does not give a distance in the usual sense,
for two reasons:
d
is not symmetric and it does not satisfy the triangle inequality.
One can show instead that
d
satisfies a multiplicative version of the triangle inequality,
namely, that for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in S
,

d(s_{1},s_{3})\leq d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})
.
This is a direct consequence of the following properties of
\precsim_{\varepsilon}
.
The first property states that
\precsim_{\varepsilon}
is monotonic with respect to
increasing values of
\varepsilon
.

D.4.6
.

If
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
and
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
, then
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{2}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
D.3.7
.
∎

The second property is a quantitative generalisation
of transitivity from which the multiplicative inequality
discussed above follows.

D.4.7
.

If
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
and
s_{2}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
, then
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
.

Proof.

Since
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
and
s_{2}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
,
there exists an
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R
such that
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R

and an
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-simulation relation
R^{\prime}
such that
(s_{2},s_{3})\in R^{\prime}
.
First construct a relation

R^{\prime\prime}=R\circ R^{\prime}=\{(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{3}^{\prime})\in S\times S\mid\exists s_{2}^{\prime}.((s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in R\text{ and }(s_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}^{\prime})\in R^{\prime})\}.

Now pick an arbitrary pair
(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{3}^{\prime})\in R^{\prime\prime}
.
Clearly there exists
s_{2}^{\prime}
such that
(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in R
and
(s_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}
.
Hence
L(s_{1}^{\prime})=L(s_{2}^{\prime})=L(s_{3}^{\prime})
and
F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)

and
F_{s_{3}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
,
so
F_{s_{3}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)
,
meaning
F_{s_{3}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}}F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}
.
Thus the first and second conditions are satisfied.

Now let
a\in A
. There exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}
between
\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)
and
\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)

and another coupling
\Delta_{a}^{\prime}
between
\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)
and
\tau(s_{3}^{\prime},a)
.
Next we construct a coupling between
\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)
and
\tau(s_{3}^{\prime},a)
by

\Delta_{a}^{\prime\prime}(s,s^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a))}\frac{\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})}{\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(s^{\prime})}.

(D.1)

We first verify that this is a coupling.

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime\prime}(s,s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2},a))}\frac{\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})}{\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2},a))}\frac{\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})}{\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2},a))}\frac{\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})}{\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2},a))}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{1},a)(s),

and likewise we can show that
\sum_{s\in S}\Delta_{a}^{\prime\prime}(s,s^{\prime\prime})=\tau(s_{3},a)(s^{\prime\prime})
.
Now assume
\Delta_{a}^{\prime\prime}(s,s^{\prime\prime})>0
.
By (
D.1
), this means that there must exist some

s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a))\quad\text{such that}\quad\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})>0\text{ and }\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})>0.

This implies that
(s,s^{\prime})\in R
and
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\in R^{\prime}
,
so by the construction of
R^{\prime\prime}
we get
(s,s^{\prime\prime})\in R^{\prime\prime}
.

Hence we have shown that
R^{\prime\prime}
is an
\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}
-simulation relation.
Since clearly
(s_{1},s_{3})\in R^{\prime\prime}
, it follows that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
.
∎

Typically, one still uses the term distance for such multiplicative distances,
because by applying the logarithm one does obtain a hemimetric.

D.4.8
.

\log d
is a hemimetric.

Proof.

Let
d^{\log}(s_{1},s_{2})=\log d(s_{1},s_{2})
.
Clearly,
d^{\log}(s_{1},s_{2})\geq 0
, and since
d(s,s)=1
,
d^{\log}(s,s)=\log(1)=0
.
Hence it only remains to verify the triangle inequality.

If
d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})=\infty
,
then clearly
d(s_{1},s_{3})\leq d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})
.
If
d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})\neq\infty
,
then the sets
\{\varepsilon\geq 1\mid s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\}
and
\{\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq 1\mid s_{2}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}\}

are both non-empty, so there must exist
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq 1

such that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
and
s_{2}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
,
so by Lemma
D.4.7
we have
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
,
and hence
d(s_{1},s_{3})\neq\infty
.
Taking the contrapositive of this,
we get that
d(s_{1},s_{3})=\infty
implies that
d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})=\infty
,
and hence also
d(s_{1},s_{3})\leq d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})
.

Now assume that
d(s_{1},s_{3})\neq\infty
and
d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})\neq\infty
,
and assume towards a contradiction that
d(s_{1},s_{3})>d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})
.
Since
d
is defined as an infimum, there must exist
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq 1
such that

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
s_{2}\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s_{3}
, and

d(s_{1},s_{3})>\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3}).

However, by Lemma
D.4.7
we have
\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq d(s_{1},s_{3})
, a contradiction.
Hence we get
d(s_{1},s_{3})\leq d(s_{1},s_{2})\cdot d(s_{2},s_{3})
, and by taking logarithms,
we get

d^{\log}(s_{1},s_{3})\leq d^{\log}(s_{1},s_{2})+d^{\log}(s_{2},s_{3}).\qed

D.4.9
.

Consider again the SMDP from Example
D.4.2
.
We can now see that
d(s_{1},s_{2})=2
and
d(s_{2},s_{1})=\frac{1}{2}
.
This also shows that our distance is not symmetric, and hence not a pseudometric.

D.4.10
.

From a topological point of view, there is no real difference between
satisfying the standard triangle inequality or its multiplicative version.
The difference essentially amounts to working either in the monoid
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},+)

or the monoid
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1},\cdot)
.
However, these monoids are isomorphic
via the bijection given by the logarithm and exponential functions. Since these
functions are also continuous, the isomorphism is actually a homeomorphism,
so all topological properties are preserved.

If one allowed
\varepsilon<1
in the distance,
then one would get strange results such as a constant sequence
which does not convergence to the constant element.
To see this, consider a sequence
\{s_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
where
s_{n}=s
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
d(s_{n},s)=1
for any element of the sequence.
However,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s

means that for any
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(s_{n},s)<\varepsilon

for all
n>N
.
However, this clearly does not hold for
0<\varepsilon<1
,
so
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\not\ni s
.

Computing the Simulation Distance

In this section we provide an algorithm to compute
the simulation distance given in Definition
D.4.5
for finite SMDPs.
The algorithm is shown to run in polynomial time for the distributions we have considered so far.

The following technical lemma will provide a sound basis for the correctness of the
algorithm. Given two CDFs
F
and
G
, let

c(F,G)=\inf\{\varepsilon\geq 1\mid F\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}G\}

denote the least acceleration factor needed by
F
to be faster than
G
.
Given an SMDP
M
, we then define

\mathcal{C}(M)=\{c(F_{s^{\prime}},F_{s})\mid s,s^{\prime}\in S\}.

D.5.1
.

Let
M
be a finite SMDP.
If
d(s_{1},s_{2})\neq\infty
, then

•

s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}
, for some
c\in\mathcal{C}(M)
and

•

d(s_{1},s_{2})=\min\{c\in\mathcal{C}(M)\mid s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}\}
.

Proof.

For the first claim, note that
d(s_{1},s_{2})\neq\infty

implies that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
for some
\varepsilon\geq 1
.
This is witnessed by some
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
which we denote by
R
.
Now let

c^{*}=\max\{c\in\mathcal{C}(M)\mid c=c_{\rho(s^{\prime}),\rho(s)}\text{ for some }(s,s^{\prime})\in R\}.

Then it is clear that
R
is also a
c^{*}
-simulation relation,
and hence
s_{1}\precsim_{c^{*}}s_{2}
.

For the second claim, let

c_{*}=\min\{c\in\mathcal{C}(M)\mid s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}\}

and

X=\{\varepsilon\geq 1\mid s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\}.

We first show that
c_{*}
is a lower bound of
X
.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
then by the previous argument we also have
