s_{1}\precsim_{c^{*}}s_{2}
.
Note that
\varepsilon\geq c^{*}
,
because otherwise we would have had

F_{s^{\prime}}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{s}
for some
(s,s^{\prime})\in R
,
contradicting the fact that
R
is a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation.
Hence

\varepsilon\geq c^{*}\geq\min\{c\in\mathcal{C}(M)\mid s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}\}=c_{*},

so
c_{*}
is a lower bound of
X
.
Next we show that
c_{*}
is the greatest lower bound of
X
.
We know that
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{*}}s_{2}
, and hence
c_{*}\in X
,
so if
\varepsilon>c_{*}
, then
\varepsilon
can not be a lower bound of
X
.
Hence
c_{*}
is the greatest lower bound of
X
,
and therefore we conclude that

c_{*}=\min\{c\in\mathcal{C}(M)\mid s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}\}=\inf X=d(s_{1},s_{2}).\qed

Lemma
D.5.1
provides a strategy for computing the simulation distance between any
two states
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
of a given SMDP
M
as follows.
First, one constructs the set
\mathcal{C}(M)
.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}
does not hold for any
c\in\mathcal{C}(M)
,
then the distance must be infinite;
otherwise, it is the smallest
c\in\mathcal{C}(M)
for which
s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}
holds.

In order for this strategy to work, we need two ingredients:
first, we should be able to compute the set
\mathcal{C}(M)
and second,
for any
c\in\mathcal{C}(M)
, we need an algorithm for checking whether
s_{1}\precsim_{c}s_{2}
.

Recall that SMDPs allow for
arbitrary
residence-time distributions in each state.
Therefore, it is not guaranteed that for any SMDP
M
the set
\mathcal{C}(M)
can be computed.
With the following definition we identify the class of SMDPs for which this can be done.

D.5.2
.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of CDFs
is
effective
if for any
F,G\in\mathcal{C}
,

c(F,G)
is computable.
An SMDP
M
is effective if
\{F_{s}\mid s\in S\}
is an effective class.

In particular, for any pair of states
s,s^{\prime}
of an effective SMDP
M
,
we can decide whether
F_{s^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{s}
by simply checking whether

\varepsilon\geq c(F_{s^{\prime}},F_{s})
.
We will denote by
f(l)
the complexity of computing
c(F_{s^{\prime}},F_{s})

for two arbitrary
s,s^{\prime}\in S
as a function of the length
l

of the representation of the residence-time distributions of
M
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
denote the class consisting of the Dirac distribution at
0

as well as uniform and exponential distributions with rational parameters.
By Propositions
D.3.2
–
D.3.6

we immediately see that
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
is an effective class,
and in fact it can be computed using only simple operations such as
multiplication, division, and taking maximum.
Hence
f(l)
has constant complexity
1
1

1

As is standard, we consider numbers to be represented as floating points of bounded size in their binary representation.
whenever
M

takes residence-time distributions from
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
.

Next we consider how to decide
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
for a given rational

\varepsilon\geq 1
.
A decision procedure can be obtained by adapting to our setting
the algorithm by Baier et al.
[
baier2000
]
for deciding the simulation preorder
between probabilistic labelled transition systems.
The algorithm from
[
baier2000
]
uses a partition refinement approach
to compute the largest simulation relation and runs in time
\mathcal{O}(mn^{7}/\log n)
for reactive systems,
where
m=|A|
is the number of actions, and
n=|S|
is the number of states.
Given
\varepsilon\geq 1
, we can proceed correspondingly to compute
\varepsilon
-similarity:
we start from the relation
R=S\times S
and update it
by removing all the pairs
(s,s^{\prime})
of states
not satisfying the conditions of Definition
D.4.1
.
This process is repeated on the resulting updated relation until
no more pairs of states are removed.
The resulting relation is the largest
\varepsilon
-simulation.
Hence, checking
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
corresponds to determining whether the pair

(s_{1},s_{2})
is contained in the relation returned by the above algorithm.
The complexity can be improved to
\mathcal{O}(m^{2}n)
by storing important information about
the previous iteration of the algorithm and use this in the current iteration
[
zhang09
, Theorem 5.2.5]
.

D.5.3
.

Let
M
be a finite and effective SMDP.
Given
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
and
\varepsilon\geq 1
, deciding whether

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n)
,
where
k=|AP|
is the number of atomic propositions.

Proof.

The algorithm for deciding
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}

is essentially the same as that for deciding untimed simulation.
Since we have assumed effectiveness,
when choosing whether to remove a pair
(s,s^{\prime})
from the current relation,
we can check the conditions on Definition
D.4.1
.
However, we also need to check whether
L(s)=L(s^{\prime})
.
For this we assume that the set of atomic propositions
\mathcal{AP}

have an ordering
\mathcal{AP}=x_{0},x_{1},\dots
,
and that
L(s)
is represented as a binary array
where the
i
th entry in the array is
1
if
x_{i}\in L(s)
,
and
0
otherwise.
Then checking whether
L(s)=L(s^{\prime})

amounts to checking whether each array has the same entries,
which can be done in time
k=|\mathcal{AP}|
.
Testing for the existence of a coupling
can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2}n)
by using the algorithm from
[
zhang09
]
,
Hence we get a time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n)
.
∎

The algorithm for computing the simulation distance
is given in Algorithm
D.5.1
.
The algorithm starts by ordering the elements of
\mathcal{C}(M)
as

c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
while removing
\infty
from the list.
Then it searches for the smallest
c_{i}
such that
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{i}}s_{2}
holds.
This is done by means of a bisection method.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{1}}s_{2}
holds, then
c_{1}
is the smallest element
such that this holds, so we return it.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}
does not hold, then, by Lemma
D.4.6
,

s_{1}\precsim_{c_{i}}s_{2}
does not hold for any
1\leq i\leq n
, so we return
\infty
.
If none of the above apply, at this point of the algorithm (line 4) we have that

s_{1}\not\precsim_{c_{1}}s_{2}
and
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}
.

We use the variables
i
and
j
, respectively, as the left and right endpoints of the bisection interval.
The bisection interval keeps track of those elements
c_{n}
for which
we still do not know whether
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}
.
At the beginning,
i=1
and
j=n
.
At line 7,
h=\left\lceil\frac{j-i}{2}\right\rceil
is used as
the decrement factor for the length of the bisection interval at each step.
Since
h>0
, the bisection interval decreases in size for each iteration.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{j-h}}s_{2}
holds, then

j-h
is the current smallest element in
\mathcal{C}(M)
for which this holds,
hence
j-h
will become the new right endpoint of the interval; otherwise
i+h
is the new
left endpoint. The bisection method stops when the endpoints meet or cross each other,
at which point we know that
s_{1}\not\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}
for all
n<j

and
s_{1}\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}
for all
n\geq j
,
and hence we return
c_{j}
.

Order the elements of
\mathcal{C}(M)\setminus\{\infty\}

such that
c_{1}<c_{2}<\dots<c_{n}
;

1

if

s_{1}\precsim_{c_{1}}s_{2}

then

return

c_{1}

;

2

else if

s_{1}\not\precsim_{c_{n}}s_{2}

then

return

\infty

;

3

else

4

i\leftarrow 1,j\leftarrow n
;

5

while

i<j

do

6

h\leftarrow\left\lceil\frac{j-i}{2}\right\rceil
;

7

if

s_{1}\precsim_{c_{j-h}}s_{2}

then

j\leftarrow j-h

;

8

else

i\leftarrow i+h

;

9

end while

10

return

c_{j}
;

11

end if

12

Algorithm D.5.1

Computing the simulation distance between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.

Computing the set
\mathcal{C}(M)
at line 1 has complexity
n^{2}f(l)
,
and sorting it can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
using mergesort.
By Theorem
D.5.3
, and since we have already computed
\mathcal{C}(M)
,
each of the
\varepsilon
-simulation checks
in lines
2
,
3
, and
8
can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}k+m^{2}n)
,
but the complexity
n^{2}k
from comparing labels only needs to be computed once.
Since the bisection interval is halved each time, the while-loop is taken at most

\log n
times. We therefore get
an overall time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n\cdot\log n)
.

D.5.4
.

Let
M
be a finite and effective SMDP. The simulation distance between any two states
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n\cdot\log n)
.

Proof.

Consider the algorithm described in Algorithm
D.5.1
.
The correctness of the algorithm is given by Lemma
D.5.1
.
We will now argue that the algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n\cdot\log n)
.
The sorting in line
1
of the algorithm can be done
in time
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
using mergesort.
The checks in line
2
and
3

each has complexity
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n)

by Theorem
D.5.3
.
However, we only need to compare labels and residence-time distributions
once and then we can store the results for future iterations.
Hence the complexity
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k))

is only incurred once, and not in each iteration.
The while loop halves the number of elements left to check
for each iteration, and hence it will loop at most
\log n
times.
Since in each iteration we make the check in line
8
,
the complexity of the while loop becomes

\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(f(l)+k)+m^{2}n\cdot\log n)
.
∎

Compositional Properties

One of the most successful principles of formal verification
is the notion of compositional reasoning,
in which a large system can be understood in terms of its smaller components
[
CLM89
]
.
However, for this principle to work,
one must ensure that properties inferred about the components
carry over to the full, composite system.
For bisimulation, this means that one wants bisimulation
to be a congruence with respect to parallel composition,
and a precongruence in the case of simulation.
A natural generalisation of this is that of non-expansiveness,
which means that parallel composition does not increase (expand) the distance between states.
In this section we will prove that some natural notions of parallel composition
on SMDPs are non-expansive with respect to the simulation distance.

First we define what it means to compose two SMDPs in parallel.
As argued in
[
SV04
]
, the style of synchronous CSP is the one that is most suitable for
SMDPs, so this is the one we will adopt here.

D.6.1
.

A function
\star:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

is a
residence-time composition function
if it is commutative.

D.6.2
.

Let
\star
be a residence-time composition function.
Then the
\star
-composition
of
M_{1}=(S_{1},\tau_{1},\rho_{1},L_{1})
and
M_{2}=(S_{2},\tau_{2},\rho_{2},L_{2})
,
denoted
M_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}M_{2}=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
, is given as follows, for
arbitrary
s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime}\in S_{1}
,
s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime}\in S_{2}
, and
a\in A
.

1.

S=S_{1}\times S_{2}
,

2.

\tau((s_{1},s_{2}),a)((s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}))=\tau_{1}(s_{1},a)(s_{1}^{\prime})\cdot\tau_{2}(s_{2},a)(s_{2}^{\prime})
,

3.

\rho((s_{1},s_{2}))=\star(\rho_{1}(s_{1}),\rho_{2}(s_{2}))
, and

4.

L((s_{1},s_{2}))=L(s_{1})\cup L(s_{2})
.

Given a composite system
M_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}M_{2}=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
,
we write
s_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{2}
to mean
(s_{1},s_{2})\in S
.
The residence-time composition function
\star
allows us to accommodate many different ways of combining timing behaviour, including those found in the literature on process algebras.
We recall here some of these.

Maximum composition:

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}(t)=\max(F_{\mu}(t),F_{\nu}(t))
.

For exponential distributions,
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
and
F_{\nu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]
,
the following alternatives can be found.

Product rate composition:

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime}\right]
.

Minimum rate composition:

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\min\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}\right]
.

Maximum rate composition:

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\max\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}\right]
.

Maximum composition is used for interactive Markov chains
[
hermanns2002
]
,
product rate composition is used in SPA
[
hermanns1998
]
,
minimum rate composition is used in PEPA
[
hillston2005
]
,
and maximum rate composition is used in TIPP
[
gotz1993
]
.

In order to have non-expansiveness for
\star
-composition of SMDPs, we will need to
restrict to residence-time composition functions
\star
that are monotonic.

D.6.3
.

A residence-time composition function
\star
is

monotonic
if for all
\varepsilon\geq 1
and

\mu,\nu,\eta\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
, it holds that

F_{\mu}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}\quad\text{implies}\quad F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}.

Requiring monotonicity is not a significant restriction,
as many of the composition functions that are found in the literature are indeed monotonic.

D.6.4
.

Maximum composition as well as product, minimum, and maximum rate composition
are all monotonic.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon\geq 1
and
assume that
F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu}(t)
for all
t
.

We first consider maximum composition.
If
F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
,
then
F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\eta}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\eta}(t)
,
so

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

On the other hand, consider the case where

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\eta}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
.
Then we know that

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu}(t)
.
If it is the case that
F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t)=F_{\nu}(t)
, then

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu}(t)=F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

If
F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t)=F_{\eta}(t)
, then

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\eta}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\eta}(t)=F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

So we conclude that
F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(t)\geq F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t^{\prime})
.

Next we consider the different rate compositions.
Assume that
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
,

F_{\nu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]
, and
F_{\eta}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta^{\prime\prime}\right]
.
Since we have assumed
F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu}(t)

for all
t
, this implies by Lemma
D.3.3
that

\varepsilon\cdot\theta\geq\theta^{\prime}
.

For product rate composition,
note that
\varepsilon\cdot\theta\geq\theta^{\prime}

implies
\varepsilon\cdot\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq\theta^{\prime}\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\varepsilon\cdot\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta^{\prime}\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\right](t)=F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

For minimum rate composition,
we want to show that

\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\geq\min\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}
.
If
\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\varepsilon\cdot\theta
,
then

\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\varepsilon\cdot\theta\geq\theta^{\prime}\geq\min\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}.

Otherwise, if
\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}
, then

\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq\min\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}.

Hence

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\min\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\right](t)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\min\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\right](t)=F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

For maximum composition, we see that if
\max\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\theta^{\prime}
, then

\max\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\geq\varepsilon\cdot\theta\geq\theta^{\prime}=\max\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\},

and if
\max\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}=\theta^{\prime\prime}
, then

\max\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\geq\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq\theta^{\prime\prime}=\max\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}.

Hence

F_{\star(\mu,\eta)}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\max\{\varepsilon\cdot\theta,\varepsilon\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\right](t)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\max\{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}\right](t)=F_{\star(\nu,\eta)}(t).

∎

Now we can prove that the
\star
-composition of finite SMDPs
is indeed non-expansive with respect to the simulation distance,
provided that
\star
is monotonic.

D.6.5
.

For finite SMDPs and monotonic
\star
,

d(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{implies}\quad d(s_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},s_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})\leq\varepsilon.

Proof.

Assume that
d(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\varepsilon
.
By Lemma
D.5.1
,
we have that
s_{1}\precsim_{d(s_{1},s_{2})}s_{2}
,
so by Lemma
D.4.6

we get that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
.
Hence, there exists a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R
such that
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
.
Now construct

R^{\prime}=\{(s_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},s_{2}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})\mid(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in R\text{ and }s_{3}\in S\},

and we want to show that
R^{\prime}
is a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation.

Pick some
(s_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},s_{2}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})\in R^{\prime}
.
Then we get

L(s_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})=L(s_{1}^{\prime})\cup L(s_{3})=L(s_{2}^{\prime})\cup L(s_{3})=L(s_{2}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}).

Since
\star
is monotonic, we immediately get

\star(\rho(s_{2}^{\prime}),\rho(s_{3}))([0,\varepsilon\cdot t])\geq\star(\rho(s_{1}^{\prime}),\rho(s_{3}))([0,t])

for all
t
, so
F_{s_{2}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{s_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}}
.
Now let
a\in A
be an arbitrary action and define

\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s_{3}^{\prime}\neq s_{3}^{\prime\prime}\\
\Delta_{a}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau(s_{3},a)(s_{3}^{\prime})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime})>0
,
then
s_{3}^{\prime}=s_{3}^{\prime\prime}
and also
\Delta_{a}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})>0
, so
(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in R
,
and hence
(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime})\in R^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{a}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau(s_{3},a)(s_{3}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{3},a)(s_{3}^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{a}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{3},a)(s_{3}^{\prime})\cdot\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(s_{1}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},a)(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime}),

and likewise we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime}}\Delta_{a}^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{2}^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},a)(s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}^{\prime\prime}).

We have thus shown that
R^{\prime}
is a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation,
and hence

s_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3}.

Clearly, this implies that
d(s_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3},s_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

We conclude this section by exploring the computational aspects of composition of SMDPs.
In particular, we would like to be able to also compute the distance between composite systems.

From Lemma
D.5.1
, we know that computing the simulation distance amounts
to being able to compute the constants
c(F_{s},F_{s^{\prime}})
,
for each pair of states
s,s^{\prime}
of the SMDP.
Hence we would like that, whenever two distributions
\mu
and
\nu
have effective CDFs then
also their composition
\star(\mu,\nu)
has an effective CDF.
By Proposition
D.3.5
, it is easy to see that this holds
for product, minimum, and maximum rate composition,
since these compositions are still exponential distributions.

For maximum composition, the class
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
is unfortunately not closed under composition.
However, the following result holds.

D.6.6
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition.
For any
\mu,\nu,\eta\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,

1.

c(F_{\mu},F_{\star(\nu,\eta)})
is computable and

2.

c(F_{\star(\mu,\eta)},F_{\nu})
is computable.

Proof.

1.

This follows from Lemmas
D.6.7
,
D.6.8
,
D.6.10
,
D.6.11
, and
D.6.12
.

2.

This follows from Lemmas
D.6.7
,
D.6.8
,
D.6.13
,
D.6.14
, and
D.6.15
. ∎

The above results tells us that if
we are interested in computing the distance
d(s_{1},s_{2}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{3})

or
d(s_{1}\mathbin{\|_{\star}}s_{2},s_{3})
,
when
\star
is maximum composition,
then we can indeed compute the constants
c
that are needed
for Algorithm
D.5.1
to work.

We now state and prove the lemmas necessary to prove Proposition
D.6.6
.

D.6.7
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\nu_{1}
, and
\nu_{2}
be measures.
The following holds for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

1.

If one of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
and one of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}

is the Dirac measure at
0
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}
.

2.

If one of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
is the Dirac measure at
0
,
but none of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}
.

3.

If none of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are the Dirac measure at
0
,
but one of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
is,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}
.

Proof.

1.

We get
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.2
.

2.

We get
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
,
so again we can use Proposition
D.3.2
.

3.

We get
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
,
and
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\neq\mathit{Dirac}\!\left[0\right]
,
so once more we can use Proposition
D.3.2
. ∎

D.6.8
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
F_{\mu_{1}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]
,
F_{\mu_{2}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
,

F_{\nu_{1}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\lambda_{1}\right]
, and
F_{\nu_{2}}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\lambda_{2}\right]
.
Then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{\max\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}}{\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}}
.
Furthermore, this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

We have four cases to consider:

1.

\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}=\theta_{1}

and
\max\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}=\lambda_{1}
,
in which case
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{1}}

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{1}}
.

2.

\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}=\theta_{2}

and
\max\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}=\lambda_{1}
,
in which case
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{1}}
.

3.

\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}=\theta_{1}

and
\max\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}=\lambda_{2}
,
in which case
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{1}}

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{2}}
.

4.

\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}=\theta_{2}

and
\max\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}=\lambda_{2}
,
in which case
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{2}}
.

In all cases, the result then follows from Proposition
D.3.5
.
∎

D.6.9
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition and let
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
and
F_{\nu}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.
If
a\leq c
and
d\leq b
, then

F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}(t)\leq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,d\right](t)
for all
t
.

Proof.

Note first that if
F_{\mu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right]
and
F_{\nu^{\prime}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right]

with
a^{\prime}\leq c^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}\leq d^{\prime}
,
then clearly
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right](t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right](t)
for all
t
.

Now,
a\leq a
and
d\leq b
, so
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,d\right](t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](t)
for all
t
.
Likewise,
a\leq c
and
d\leq d
, so
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,d\right]\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
for all
t
.
Hence

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,d\right](t)\geq\max\{\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](t),\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)\}=F_{\star(\mu,\nu)}.\qed

D.6.10
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
,
F_{\nu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},d_{1}\right]
,
and
F_{\nu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},d_{2}\right]
.

1.

If
\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}=0
and
a>0
,
then
F_{\mu}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

for any
\varepsilon
.

2.

If
\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}=0
and
a=0
,
then
F_{\mu}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}}
.

3.

If
\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}>0
, then

F_{\mu}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

where

\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{a}{\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}},\frac{b}{\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}}\right\}.

In all cases, this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

Take
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon}>0
to get

F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0.

However, since
\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}=0
,
at least one of
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},d_{1}\right](t)>0
and
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},d_{2}\right](t)>0

must hold, and hence
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)>0
.

2.

We get

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}}{b},\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[0,\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:unifineq}}.

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{b}{\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}}
, then

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}F_{\mu}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\})

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\})

\displaystyle<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\left(\frac{b}{\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}}\cdot\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\right)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},d_{1}\right](\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}),\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},d_{2}\right](\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\})\}

\displaystyle=F_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}(\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}).

3.

We consider each case separately.

Case
c_{1}\leq c_{2}
and
d_{1}\leq d_{2}
:

In this case we have
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{1}}
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.5
.

Case
c_{1}\leq c_{2}
and
d_{1}>d_{2}
:

In this case we get
\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{a}{c_{1}},\frac{b}{d_{2}}\right\}
.
If
\varepsilon=\frac{a}{c_{1}}
,
then
\frac{c_{1}}{a}\leq\frac{d_{2}}{b}
, so

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},b\cdot\frac{d_{2}}{b}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},b\cdot\frac{d_{2}}{b}\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},d_{2}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:unifineq}}.

For any
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{a}{c_{1}}
,
let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}>c_{1}
.
Then
F_{\mu}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=F_{\mu}(a)=0
,
but
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)>0

since
c_{1}\leq c_{2}
and
t>c_{1}
.

On the other hand, if
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d_{2}}
,
then
\frac{d_{2}}{b}\leq\frac{c_{1}}{a}
.
This means that

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{d_{2}}{b},d_{2}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{c_{1}}{a},d_{2}\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{1},d_{2}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:unifineq}}.

For any
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{b}{d_{2}}

we get

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d_{2})

\displaystyle<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\frac{b}{d_{2}}\cdot d_{2})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(d_{2})

because
d_{1}>d_{2}
.

Case
c_{1}>c_{2}
and
d_{1}\leq d_{2}
:

In this case we get
\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{a}{c_{2}},\frac{b}{d_{1}}\right\}
.
If
\varepsilon=\frac{a}{c_{2}}
,
then
\frac{c_{2}}{a}\leq\frac{d_{1}}{b}
, and hence

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},b\cdot\frac{c_{2}}{a}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},b\cdot\frac{d_{1}}{b}\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},d_{1}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:unifineq}}.

For any
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{a}{c_{2}}
,
let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}>c_{2}

in order to get
F_{\mu}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0
,
but
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)>0
since
c_{1}>c_{2}
and
t>c_{2}
.

On the other hand, if
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d_{1}}
,
then
\frac{d_{1}}{b}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{a}
.
Then we get

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{d_{1}}{b},d_{1}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c_{2},d_{1}\right](t)

\displaystyle\geq F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:unifineq}}.

For any
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{b}{d_{1}}
we get

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d_{1})

\displaystyle<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\frac{b}{d_{1}}\cdot d_{1})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(d_{1})

since
d_{1}\leq d_{2}
.

Case
c_{1}>c_{2}
and
d_{1}>d_{2}
:

In this case we have
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=F_{\nu_{2}}
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.5
. ∎

D.6.11
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
,
F_{\nu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
,
F_{\nu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.
Then
F_{\mu}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

for any
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\min\{b,d\})=1
,
but
F_{\mu}(t)<1
for all
t
,
so

F_{\mu}(\varepsilon\cdot\min\{b,d\})<F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\min\{b,d\})

for any
\varepsilon
.
∎

D.6.12
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
\mu_{1}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]
,
\mu_{2}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
,

\nu_{1}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
, and
\nu_{2}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.

1.

F_{\mu_{1}}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}
for any
\varepsilon
.

2.

If
a>0
, then
F_{\mu_{2}}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

for any
\varepsilon
.

3.

If
a=0
and
\frac{1}{d-c}\geq\theta_{2}
,
then
F_{\mu_{2}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d}
.

4.

If
a=0
and
\frac{1}{d-c}<\theta_{2}
,
then
F_{\mu_{2}}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

where
\varepsilon=\theta_{2}\cdot b
.

In all cases, this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(d)=1
,
but
F_{\mu_{1}}(t)<1
for all
t
,
so
F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon\cdot d)<F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(d)
for any
\varepsilon
.

2.

If
a>0
, let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be given.
Let
t=\frac{a}{\varepsilon}>0
to get

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t)>0

since
t>0
, but

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](a)=0
,
and hence
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)<F_{\llbracket\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rrbracket}(t)
.

3.

If
a=0
and
\frac{1}{d-c}\geq\theta_{2}
,
then the slope of
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
is greater than that of
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]

until
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
hits
1
and flattens out.
Hence
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.5

to get the result.

4.

If
a=0
and
\frac{1}{d-c}<\theta_{2}
,
let
\varepsilon=\theta_{2}\cdot b
.
By Proposition
D.3.6

we then get
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)

for all
t
.
Since the slope of
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a}{\varepsilon},\frac{b}{\varepsilon}\right]

is
\theta_{2}
, it has greater slope than
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
,
and hence also

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a}{\varepsilon},\frac{b}{\varepsilon}\right](t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
.
We therefore get

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\max\{\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t),\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)\}=F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t).

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\theta_{2}\cdot b
,
then the slope in
0
of

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{\prime}},\frac{b}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right](t)

must be less than that of
\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
.
Hence there exists some
t>0

sufficiently close to
0
such that

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t)=F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(t)
. ∎

D.6.13
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
F_{\mu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{1}\right]
,
F_{\mu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right]
,
and
F_{\nu}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.

1.

If
c=0
and
\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}>0
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

for any
\varepsilon
.

2.

If
c=0
and
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{\min\{b_{1},b_{2}\}}{d}
.

3.

If
c=0
and
\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}=0

and either
a_{1}<a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
or
a_{1}>a_{2}
and
b_{1}>b_{2}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{\min\{b_{1},b_{2}\}}{d}
.

4.

If
c=0
and
\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}=0

and either
a_{1}<a_{2}
and
b_{1}>b_{2}
or
a_{1}>a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{\max\{b_{1},b_{2}\}}{d}
.

5.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}<a_{2}
,
b_{1}>b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{1}\cdot c}{b_{1}}}\geq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{2}\cdot d}{b_{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\min\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{c},\frac{b_{2}}{d}\right\}
.

6.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}<a_{2}
,
b_{1}>b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{1}\cdot c}{b_{1}}}<\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{2}\cdot d}{b_{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{b_{2}}{d}
.

7.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}<a_{2}
,
b_{1}>b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{1}\cdot c}{b_{1}}}\geq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{2}\cdot d}{b_{2}}}>\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{a_{1}}{c}
.

8.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}<a_{2}
,
b_{1}>b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{1}\cdot c}{b_{1}}}<\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{2}\cdot d}{b_{2}}}>\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where

\varepsilon=\frac{(b_{1}-a_{1})\cdot k}{d\cdot k-c\cdot k-d\cdot a_{1}+b_{1}\cdot c}

and
k=\frac{a_{1}\cdot b_{2}-a_{2}\cdot b_{1}}{a_{1}-b_{1}-a_{2}+b_{2}}
.

9.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}>a_{2}
,
b_{1}<b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{2}\cdot c}{b_{2}}}\geq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{1}\cdot d}{b_{1}}}\leq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\min\left\{\frac{a_{2}}{c},\frac{b_{1}}{d}\right\}
.

10.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}>a_{2}
,
b_{1}<b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{2}\cdot c}{b_{2}}}<\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{1}\cdot d}{b_{1}}}\leq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{b_{1}}{d}
.

11.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}>a_{2}
,
b_{1}<b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{2}\cdot c}{b_{2}}}\geq\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{1}\cdot d}{b_{1}}}>\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{a_{2}}{c}
.

12.

If
c>0
,
a_{1}>a_{2}
,
b_{1}<b_{2}
,

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{2}\cdot c}{b_{2}}}<\frac{1}{d-c}
,
and
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{1}\cdot d}{b_{1}}}>\frac{1}{d-c}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where

\varepsilon=\frac{(b_{2}-a_{2})\cdot k}{d\cdot k-c\cdot k-d\cdot a_{2}+b_{2}\cdot c}

and
k=\frac{a_{1}\cdot b_{2}-a_{2}\cdot b_{1}}{a_{1}-b_{1}-a_{2}+b_{2}}
.

13.

Otherwise,

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu}

where

\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}}{c},\frac{\min\{b_{1},b_{2}\}}{d}\right\}.

In all cases, this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

Take an arbitrary
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

and let
t=\frac{\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}}{\varepsilon}>0
.
Then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\})=0
,
but
F_{\nu}(t)>0
since
c=0
and
t>0
.
Hence
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)<F_{\nu}(t)
.

2.

If
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[0,\min\{b_{1},b_{2}\}\right]
,
so the result follows from Proposition
D.3.5
.

3.

If
a_{1}\leq a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{1}}
,
and if
a_{1}>a_{2}
and
b_{1}>b_{2}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}
.
In either case, we can then use Proposition
D.3.5

to obtain the result.

4.

We consider here the case where
a_{1}<a_{2}
and
b_{1}>b_{2}
.
The case where
a_{1}>a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
is symmetrical,
noting that if
b_{1}=b_{2}
, then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{1}}
,
in which case the result follows from Proposition
D.3.5
.
We have
c=\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}=a_{1}=0

and
\varepsilon=\frac{b_{1}}{d}
.
Then

\displaystyle F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{1}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1}\cdot\frac{d}{b_{1}},b_{1}\cdot\frac{d}{b_{1}}\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[0,d\right](t)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

To see that this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds,
first note that

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a_{1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{b_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right]
and

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{b_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right]

cross in the point

t=\frac{a_{1}\cdot b_{2}-a_{2}\cdot b_{1}}{\varepsilon\cdot(a_{1}-b_{1}-a_{2}+b_{2})}

with
0=a_{1}<a_{2}<t<b_{2}<b_{1}
.
From this it follows that

1>\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)>0.

Hence, if
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
we get

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{1}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)>\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)

and

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)>\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t),

and therefore
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
.

5.

\frac{1}{c-\frac{a_{1}\cdot c}{b_{1}}}\geq\frac{1}{d-c}

means that

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{2}\right]\left(\frac{a_{1}}{c}\cdot t\right)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,b_{1}\cdot\frac{c}{a_{1}}\right](t)

has greater slope than
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
, so

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{1}\right]\left(\frac{a_{1}}{c}\cdot t\right)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

Likewise,
\frac{1}{d-\frac{a_{2}\cdot d}{b_{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{d-c}

means that

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right]\left(\frac{b_{2}}{d}\cdot t\right)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2}\cdot\frac{d}{b_{2}},d\right](t)

has smaller slope than
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
,
and hence

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right](\frac{b_{2}}{d}\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

We therefore conclude

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\left(\min\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{c},\frac{b_{2}}{d}\right\}\cdot t\right)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t).

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
,
first assume that
\varepsilon=\frac{a_{1}}{c}

and let
t=\frac{a_{1}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}>c
.
Then

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(a_{1})=0,

but
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)>0
since
t>c
.
Now assume that
\varepsilon=\frac{b_{2}}{d}
.
Then we get

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d)<F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))}(\varepsilon\cdot d)=F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(b_{2})=1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d).

6.

This case is the same as case 5,
only considering the part where
\varepsilon=\frac{b_{2}}{d}
.

7.

This case is the same as case 5,
only considering the part where
\varepsilon=\frac{a_{1}}{c}
.

8.

\varepsilon
and
k
are chosen such that

\frac{k}{\varepsilon}
is the point in which

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a_{1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{b_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right]
,

\mathit{Unif}\!\left[\frac{a_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{b_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right]
,
and
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
cross.
Hence we get

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\left(\varepsilon\cdot\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]\left(\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right).

We have

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{1},b_{1}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)

for any
t\geq\frac{k}{\varepsilon}
and

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a_{2},b_{2}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)

for any
t\leq\frac{k}{\varepsilon}
.
Hence we can conclude that
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](t)
.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\left(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right)<F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\left(\varepsilon\cdot\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]\left(\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right)
.

9.

Symmetric to case 5.

10.

Symmetric to case 6.

11.

Symmetric to case 7.

12.

Symmetric to case 8.

13.

In this case,
we either get
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{1}}

or
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}
,
and the result is then obtained by applying Proposition
D.3.5
. ∎

D.6.14
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
F_{\mu}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
,
F_{\nu_{1}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
,
F_{\nu_{2}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.

1.

If
\min\{a,c\}>0
, then

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\not\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu}
for any
\varepsilon
.

2.

If
a=0
and
c>0
, then

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu}

where
\varepsilon=\theta\cdot b
.

3.

If
a>0
and
c=0
, then

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu}

where
\varepsilon=\theta\cdot d
.

4.

If
a=0
and
c=0
, then

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\mu}

where
\varepsilon=\theta\cdot\min\{b,d\}
.

In all cases, this is the least
\varepsilon
such that
the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

If
\min\{a,c\}>0
, let
\varepsilon
be given,
and let
t=\frac{\min\{a,c\}}{\varepsilon}>0
. Then

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\min\{a,c\})=0

but
F_{\mu}(t)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t)>0
since
t>0
.

2.

By Proposition
D.3.6
,
we know that
\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right]
.
Hence

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t).

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\theta\cdot b
,
then there must exist some
t>0
sufficiently close to
0

such that

F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta\right](t).

3.

Similar to the case where
a=0
and
c>0
.

4.

If
a=0
and
c=0
,
then we get
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
if
b\leq d

and
F_{\star(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
if
b>d
.
In either case,
we can use Proposition
D.3.6
to obtain the desired result. ∎

D.6.15
.

Let
\star
be maximum composition,
and let
\mu_{1}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]
,
\mu_{2}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
,

\nu_{1}=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right]
, and
\nu_{2}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right]
.

1.

If
a=0
and
\frac{1}{b-a}\geq\theta_{1}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{1}}

where
\varepsilon=\theta_{2}\cdot b
.

2.

If
a>0
or
\frac{1}{b-a}<\theta_{1}
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{1}}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}
.

3.

If
\frac{1}{b-a}\geq\theta_{1}
and
a=0
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{2}}

where
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d}
.

4.

Otherwise,
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqsubseteq_{\varepsilon}F_{\nu_{2}}
where

\varepsilon=\max\left\{\frac{b}{d},\frac{(b-a)\cdot k}{d\cdot k-c\cdot k-d\cdot a+b\cdot c}\right\},

k=\frac{b\cdot\theta_{1}+W(\theta_{1}\cdot(a-b)\cdot e^{-b\cdot\theta_{1}})}{\theta_{1}}
,
and
W
is the Lambert
W
-function.

In all cases, this is the least
\varepsilon

such that the
\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds.

Proof.

1.

In this case,
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.6
to obtain the result.

2.

We get

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}\cdot t\right)\geq\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right]\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}\cdot t\right)=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t)=F_{\nu_{1}}(t).

To see that this is the least
\varepsilon
such that the

\varepsilon
-faster-than relation holds,
first note that because
a>0
or
\frac{1}{b-a}<\theta_{1}
,
there must be some interval
[0,t]

where
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(t^{\prime})=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right](t^{\prime})

for all
t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
,
then let
t^{\prime}\in\left[0,\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right]
,
so that
\varepsilon\cdot t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
,
and also
\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
.
Then we get

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime})=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime})<\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{1}\right](\varepsilon\cdot t^{\prime})=\mathit{Exp}\!\left[\theta_{2}\right](t^{\prime}).

3.

We get
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=F_{\mu_{2}}
,
so we can use Proposition
D.3.5
.

4.

In this case,
F_{\mu_{1}}
and
F_{\mu_{2}}

will cross in some non-zero point.

k
is chosen so that

F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon^{*}\cdot t^{*})=F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon^{*}\cdot t^{*})=F_{\nu_{2}}(t^{*})

where

\varepsilon^{*}=\frac{(b-a)\cdot k}{d\cdot k-c\cdot k-d\cdot a+b\cdot c}

and

t^{*}=\frac{k}{\varepsilon^{*}}.

This also means that

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)=\begin{cases}F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)&\text{if }t\leq t^{*}\\
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)&\text{if }t\geq t^{*}.\end{cases}

Now, if
\varepsilon=\frac{b}{d}
,
then
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot d)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a\cdot\frac{d}{b},d\right](d)=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d)
,
and
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t^{*})\geq F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon^{*}\cdot t^{*})=F_{\nu_{2}}(t^{*})
.
Hence
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)
for all
t\geq t^{*}
.
For
t\leq t^{*}
we get
F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)
,
and hence
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)
.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot d)<F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot d)=1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d)
.

If
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{*}
,
then
F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon\cdot t^{*})=F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t^{*})=F_{\nu_{2}}(t^{*})
.
Since

F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot d)\geq F_{\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{b}{d}\cdot d\right)=1=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[c,d\right](d),

we get
F_{\mu_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)

for all
t\geq t^{*}
.
For
t\leq t^{*}
we get
F_{\mu_{1}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)
,
and hence we conclude

F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{\nu_{2}}(t)
.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
,
then
F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t^{*})<F_{\star(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(\varepsilon\cdot t^{*})=F_{\nu_{2}}(t^{*})
. ∎

Logical Properties of the Simulation Distance

If the distance between two processes is small,
then we would also expect that they satisfy almost the same properties.
In order to make this idea precise,
in this section we introduce and study a slight extension of Markovian logic
[
KMP13
]
,
which we will call
timed Markovian logic
(
\mathrm{TML}
).
The syntax of
\mathrm{TML}
is given by the following grammar,
where
\alpha\in\mathcal{AP}
,
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\cap[0,1]
,
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, and
a\in A
.

\mathrm{TML}:\quad\varphi::=\alpha\mid\neg\alpha\mid\ell_{p}t\mid m_{p}t\mid L_{p}^{a}\varphi\mid M_{p}^{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\land\varphi^{\prime}\mid\varphi\lor\varphi^{\prime}

The semantics of
\mathrm{TML}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{l l l l l l l}s\models\alpha&\;\text{iff}&\alpha\in L(s)&&s\models\ell_{p}t&\;\text{iff}&F_{s}(t)\geq p\\
s\models\neg\alpha&\;\text{iff}&\alpha\notin L(s)&&s\models m_{p}t&\;\text{iff}&F_{s}(t)\leq p\\
s\models\varphi\land\varphi^{\prime}&\;\text{iff}&s\models\varphi\text{ and }s\models\varphi^{\prime}&&s\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi&\;\text{iff}&\tau(s,a)(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket)\geq p\\
s\models\varphi\lor\varphi^{\prime}&\;\text{iff}&s\models\varphi\text{ or }s\models\varphi^{\prime}&&s\models M_{p}^{a}\varphi&\;\text{iff}&\tau(s,a)(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket)\leq p\\
\end{array}

where
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket=\{s\in S\mid s\models\varphi\}

is the set of states satisfying
\varphi
.

We also isolate the following two fragments of
\mathrm{TML}
.

\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}:\quad\varphi::=\alpha\mid\neg\alpha\mid\ell_{p}t\mid L_{p}^{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\land\varphi^{\prime}\mid\varphi\lor\varphi^{\prime}

\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}:\quad\varphi::=\alpha\mid\neg\alpha\mid m_{p}t\mid M_{p}^{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\land\varphi^{\prime}\mid\varphi\lor\varphi^{\prime}

Intuitively, the modal formula
L_{p}^{a}\varphi
says
that the probability of taking an
a
-transition to where
\varphi
holds is
at least

p
,
and
M_{p}^{a}\varphi
says the probability is
at most

p
.

\ell_{p}t
and
m_{p}t
are similar in spirit,
but talk about the probability of firing a transition instead.
Thus,
\ell_{p}t
says that the probability
of firing a transition before time
t
is
at least

p
,
whereas
m_{p}t
says that the probability is
at most

p
.

For any
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}
and
\varepsilon\geq 1

we denote the
\varepsilon
-perturbation
of
\varphi
by
(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}

and define it inductively as

\begin{array}[]{l l l}(\alpha)_{\varepsilon}=\alpha&&(\ell_{p}t)_{\varepsilon}=\ell_{p}\varepsilon\cdot t\\
(\neg\alpha)_{\varepsilon}=\neg\alpha&&(m_{p}t)_{\varepsilon}=m_{p}\varepsilon\cdot t\\
(\varphi\land\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}=(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\land(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}&&(L_{p}^{a}\varphi)_{\varepsilon}=L_{p}^{a}(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\\
(\varphi\lor\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}=(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\lor(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}&&(M_{p}^{a}\varphi)_{\varepsilon}=M_{p}^{a}(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}.\\
\end{array}

By making use of the alternative characterisation
for simulation given in Proposition
D.2.3

and drawing upon ideas from
[
desharnais99
]
,
we can now prove the following logical characterisation of the
\varepsilon
-simulation relation.

D.7.1
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
with
\varepsilon\geq 1
.
Then the following holds.

•

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\quad
if and only if
\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}.s_{1}\models\varphi\implies s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}
.

•

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\quad
if and only if
\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}.s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\implies s_{1}\models\varphi
.

Proof.

\bullet
We first prove the first item.

(
\implies
)
We proceed by induction on
\varphi
.
The cases of conjunction and disjunction are standard.

Case
\varphi=\alpha
:
s_{1}\models\alpha
means that
\alpha\in L(s_{1})
.
Since
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
we then get
s_{2}\models\alpha
.

Case
\varphi=\neg\alpha
:
s_{1}\models\neg\alpha
means that
\alpha\notin L(s_{1})
.
Since
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
we then get
s_{2}\models\neg\alpha
.

Case
\varphi=\ell_{p}t
:
s_{1}\models\ell_{p}t
means that
F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq p
,
and since
F_{s_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)
, we get
s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}
.

Case
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
:
s_{1}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}

means that
\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p
.
There exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{1},a)(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

(ind. hyp.)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket),

and hence
s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}
.

(
\impliedby
)
We construct the relation

\mathcal{R}=\{(s,s^{\prime})\in S\times S\mid\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}.s\models\varphi\implies s^{\prime}\models\varphi\}

and we must show that it is a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation.
Let
(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
be arbitrary.
We will first show that
L(s_{1}^{\prime})=L(s_{2}^{\prime})
.
If
\alpha\in L(s_{1}^{\prime})
, then
s_{1}^{\prime}\models\alpha
,
and hence
s_{2}^{\prime}\models\alpha
, which means that
\alpha\in L(s_{2}^{\prime})
.
If
\alpha\notin L(s_{1}^{\prime})
, then
s_{1}^{\prime}\models\neg\alpha
,
implying that
s_{2}^{\prime}\models\neg\alpha
, so
\alpha\notin L(s_{2}^{\prime})
.
Therefore
L(s_{1}^{\prime})=L(s_{2}^{\prime})
.

Next we will show that
F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\leq F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Assume towards a contradiction that

F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)>F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
for some
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.
Then there exists
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
such that

F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)>q>F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
.
But then
s_{1}^{\prime}\models\ell_{q}t
whereas
s_{2}^{\prime}\not\models\ell_{q}\varepsilon\cdot t
,
which contradicts how
\mathcal{R}
was constructed.
Hence
F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\leq F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.
Now assume towards a contradiction that
F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)>F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

for some
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

and let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)-F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)>0
.
By right-continuity, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\varepsilon\cdot t<c<\varepsilon\cdot t+\delta

implies
|F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(c)-F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)|<\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
Now pick some
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}

such that
\varepsilon\cdot t<q<\varepsilon\cdot t+\delta
.
Then

F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\leq F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(q)<F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\leq F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(q),

which is a contradiction.
Hence we conclude that
F_{s_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\leq F_{s_{2}^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Finally we will show that
\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(R(C))
for all
a
and
C\subseteq S
.
Pick an arbitrary
a
and
C\subseteq S
.
By construction of
\mathcal{R}
, we know that

\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket)\geq p\quad\text{implies}\quad\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\llbracket(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket)\geq p\quad\text{for any }p.

This implies that

\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\llbracket(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket)

(D.2)

for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
.
The strategy will now be to construct a formula
\varphi

to exploit the inequality in Equation (
D.2
).
To do this, we introduce the following notation.
For a state
s\in S
, let

\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}\mid s\models\varphi\}\text{ and }\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis^{\varepsilon}=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}\mid s\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\}.

Given a formula
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
,
we let
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
be the set of values
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}

and
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\cap[0,1]
that are used in
\varphi
.
Furthermore, we define the depth
0pt(\varphi)
as

0pt(\varphi)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\varphi=\ell_{p}t\text{ or }\varphi=m_{p}t,\\
1+0pt(\varphi^{\prime})&\text{if }\parbox[t]{103.50105pt}{$\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}$, $\varphi=M_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}$, or $\varphi=\neg\varphi^{\prime}$,}\\
1+\max\{0pt(\varphi_{1}),0pt(\varphi_{2})\}&\text{if }\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}.\end{cases}

Finally, we let

I_{k}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\mid q=l\cdot\frac{1}{j}\text{ for some }l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\text{ and }j\in\mathbb{N}\text{ where }l\leq k\text{ and }j\leq k\}.

Then we can define

F_{k}=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}\mid 0pt(\varphi)\leq k\text{ and }\mathbb{Q}\subseteq I_{k}\}

as a finite fragment of
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
and

\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k}=\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis\cap F_{k}=\{\varphi\in F_{k}\mid s\models\varphi\}

as the restriction of
\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis
to
F_{k}
.
Intuitively,
F_{k}
is a better and better approximation of all formulas of
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}

as
k
increases.
Formally, this means that
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}F_{k}=\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
,
and hence any formula in
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
will be in one of the
F_{k}
for some
k
.
Now we can construct a formula that describes the set
R(C)
.
Note that by construction of
\mathcal{R}
we have

\mathcal{R}(C)=\{s^{\prime}\in S\mid\exists s\in C.(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}\}=\{s^{\prime}\in S\mid\exists s\in C.\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis\subseteq\llparenthesis s^{\prime}\rrparenthesis^{\varepsilon}\}

and we also have

\bigcup_{s\in C}\bigcap_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}\llbracket(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket=\bigcup_{s\in C}\{s^{\prime}\in S\mid\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis\subseteq\llparenthesis s^{\prime}\rrparenthesis^{\varepsilon}\}=\{s^{\prime}\in S\mid\exists s\in C.\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis\subseteq\llparenthesis s^{\prime}\rrparenthesis^{\varepsilon}\},

so
\mathcal{R}(C)=\bigcup_{s\in C}\bigcap_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}\llbracket(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket
.

We consider the case where
C
is finite and the case where
C
is infinite separately.
Assume first that
C
is finite.
Now let

\chi^{C}_{k}=\bigvee_{s\in C}\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k}}\varphi,\quad(\chi^{C}_{k})_{\varepsilon}=\bigvee_{s\in C}\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k}}(\varphi)_{\varepsilon},\quad\text{and}

\chi^{C}=\bigvee_{s\in C}\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}\varphi.

Because
C
is finite,
\chi^{C}_{k}
,
(\chi^{C}_{k})_{\varepsilon}
, and
\chi^{C}

consist of finitely many disjunctions and conjunctions and are hence formulas.
These formulas will be the ones we use in Equation (
D.2
).
Note that
\llbracket\chi^{C}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\chi^{C}_{k}\rrbracket
for any
k
.
Now let

C_{k}=\llbracket\chi^{C}_{k}\rrbracket\quad\text{and}\quad C^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\llbracket(\chi^{C}_{k})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket.

Then we get decreasing chains

C_{1}\supseteq C_{2}\supseteq\dots\quad\text{and}\quad C^{\varepsilon}_{1}\supseteq C^{\varepsilon}_{2}\supseteq\dots

of finite sets, and we will now prove that
\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\mathcal{R}(C)
.
If
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(C)
,
then there exists
s\in C
such that
\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis\subseteq\llparenthesis s^{\prime}\rrparenthesis^{\varepsilon}
,
and hence
s^{\prime}\models\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k}}(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}

for all
k
, so
s^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{\varepsilon}_{k}
.
If
s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{R}(C)
,
then for all
s\in C
there exists
\varphi_{s}\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}

such that
s\models\varphi_{s}
but
s^{\prime}\not\models(\varphi_{s})_{\varepsilon}
.
Since
C
is finite,
we can fix
k^{\prime}
such that
\varphi_{s}\in F_{k^{\prime}}
for all
s\in C
.
Then
s^{\prime}\notin C^{\varepsilon}_{k^{\prime}}
because

s^{\prime}\not\models\bigvee_{s\in C}\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k^{\prime}}}(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}

since
\varphi_{s}\in\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis_{k^{\prime}}
.
Therefore
s^{\prime}\notin\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{\varepsilon}_{k}
,
so we conclude that
\mathcal{R}(C)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{\varepsilon}_{k}
.

Now, by Equation (
D.2
), we get

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\implies{}}\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)\left(\llbracket\chi^{C}_{k}\rrbracket\right)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)\left(\llbracket(\chi^{C}_{k})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle\implies\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C_{k})\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(C^{\varepsilon}_{k})

\displaystyle\text{for all }k

\displaystyle\implies\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C_{k^{\prime}})\leq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(C^{\varepsilon}_{k})

\displaystyle\text{for a fixed }k^{\prime}

\displaystyle\implies\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C_{k^{\prime}})\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)\left(\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{\varepsilon}_{k}\right)

(cont. of measures)

\displaystyle\implies\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(\llbracket\chi^{C}\rrbracket)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\mathcal{R}(C))

\displaystyle\llbracket\chi^{C}\rrbracket\subseteq C_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\implies\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\mathcal{R}(C))

\displaystyle C\subseteq\llbracket\chi^{C}\rrbracket.

Next assume that
C
is countably infinite and let
\{C_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

be an increasing sequence of finite sets such that
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C_{k}=C
.
Since every
C_{k}
is finite, we get

\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C_{k})\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\mathcal{R}(C_{k}))

from what we just proved for the finite case.
By continuity of measures, this implies

\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)(C)=\tau(s_{1}^{\prime},a)\left(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C_{k}\right)\leq\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)\left(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{R}(C_{k})\right)=\tau(s_{2}^{\prime},a)(\mathcal{R}(C)).

\bullet
We now prove the second item.

(
\implies
)
For this we first prove that

s_{1}\precsim s_{2}\quad\text{implies}\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}.s_{2}\models\varphi\implies s_{1}\models\varphi

by induction on
\varphi
.
We only consider here the cases of
\varphi=m_{p}t
and
\varphi=M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
,
since the other cases are as in the first item.

Case
\varphi=m_{p}t
:
s_{2}\models m_{p}t
means that
F_{s_{2}}(t)\leq p
.
Since
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
, we know that
F_{s_{1}}(t)\leq F_{s_{2}}(t)
,
so
F_{s_{2}}(t)\leq p
, implying
s_{1}\models m_{p}t
.

Case
\varphi=M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
:
s_{2}\models M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
means that
\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\leq p
.
By induction hypothesis, we know that
\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket
is
\precsim
-closed,
and hence we get
\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\leq\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)
,
so
s_{1}\models M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
.

We now prove the claim that

s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}\quad\text{implies}\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}.s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\implies s_{1}\models\varphi

by induction on
\varphi
.
The only case left to consider is the case where
\varphi=M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
,
since the remaining cases are as before

Case
\varphi=M^{a}_{p}\varphi^{\prime}
:
We have
\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket)\leq p
.
Now,
\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket)=\tau((s_{2})_{\varepsilon},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)
,
and since we now know that
\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket
is
\precsim
-closed, we get

p\geq\tau((s_{2})_{\varepsilon},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket),

meaning that
s_{1}\models M^{a}_{r}\varphi^{\prime}
.

(
\impliedby
) Same as the first item.
∎

As a special case of Theorem
D.7.1
,
we have also shown that
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
and
\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}
characterise simulation for SMDPs.
Conceptually, Theorem
D.7.1
says
that if
s_{1}

\varepsilon
-simulates
s_{2}
,
then
s_{2}
satisfies the
\varepsilon
-perturbation of any property that
s_{2}
satisfies
for the
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
fragment of
\mathrm{TML}
,
and vice versa for the
\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}
fragment.

By Lemma
D.5.1
and Theorem
D.7.1
,
we get the following corollary,
connecting our simulation distance with the properties
expressible in the logic
\mathrm{TML}
.

D.7.2
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
with
\varepsilon\geq 1
.
For finite SMDPs the following holds.

•

d(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\varepsilon\quad
if and only if
\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}.s_{1}\models\varphi\implies s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}
.

•

d(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\varepsilon\quad
if and only if
\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}.s_{2}\models(\varphi)_{\varepsilon}\implies s_{1}\models\varphi
.

By Proposition
D.2.4
,
we also get a logical characterisation of bisimulation for SMDPs in terms of
\mathrm{TML}
,
which is simpler than the one given in
[
neuhausser2007
,
SZG14
]
.

D.7.3
.

s_{1}\sim s_{2}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}.\;s_{1}\models\varphi\iff s_{2}\models\varphi.

Proof.

(
\implies
)
We first prove that
s_{1}\models\varphi
implies
s_{2}\models\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}
.
The proof proceeds by induction on
\varphi
.
The cases of disjunction and conjunction are standard,
and the cases of
\varphi=\alpha
and
\varphi=\neg\alpha

are the same as in the proof of Theorem
B.6.1
.

Case
\varphi=\ell_{p}t
:
s_{1}\models\ell_{p}t
means
F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq p
,
and since
F_{s_{1}}(t)=F_{s_{2}}(t)
, we get
s_{2}\models\ell_{p}t
.

Case
\varphi=m_{p}t
: Same argument as
\ell_{p}t
.

Case
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
:
s_{1}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}

means
\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p
.
We know that there exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}
such that

\displaystyle\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{1},a)(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

(ind. hyp.)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket),

so
s_{2}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.

Case
\varphi=M_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
: Same argument as
L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.

Next we prove that
s_{2}\models\varphi

implies
s_{1}\models\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}
,
again by induction on
\varphi
.
All cases except
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
and
\varphi=M_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
are as before.

Case
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
:
We have
\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p
.
Since
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
, in particular we have
s_{2}\precsim s_{1}
,
so by Theorem
D.7.1
and Proposition
D.2.3
we get

\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\leq\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket),

and hence
s_{1}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.

Case
\varphi=M_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
: Same argument as
L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.

(
\impliedby
)
We have assumed that
\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}.s_{1}\models\varphi\iff s_{2}\models\varphi
,
and hence we also get

\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}.s_{1}\models\varphi\implies s_{2}\models\varphi
and

\forall\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}.s_{2}\models\varphi\implies s_{1}\models\varphi
.
By Theorem
B.6.1
we then get
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
and
s_{2}\precsim s_{1}
,
and hence Proposition
D.2.4
implies
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
.
∎

With the logical characterisation of
\varepsilon
-simulation in hand,
we can now prove the promised result that the kernel of the simulation distance is simulation.
For this, we first need the following technical lemma.
Given a weight function
\Delta_{a}
, we let

\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})=\{(s,s^{\prime})\in S\times S\mid\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})>0\}.

D.7.4
.

Assume that
M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
is finitely supported
and consider
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
for all
\varepsilon>1
then
for all
\varepsilon>1
and
a\in L
there exists a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon}

and a weight function
\Delta_{a}
such that for any
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>1

there exists a
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}

with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>1
and
a\in L
. Because we know that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
there exists a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon}
and a weight function
\Delta_{a}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon}
.
Now let
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{1}>1
.
Because
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon_{1}}s_{2}
,
we know that there exists a
\varepsilon_{1}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{1}}
and a weight function
\Delta_{a}^{1}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{1}}
.

If
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})=\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})
, we are done.
If not, it may be the case that for any
\varepsilon_{1}>\varepsilon_{2}>1
there exists a
\varepsilon_{2}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{2}}

and a weight function
\Delta_{a}^{2}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
.
This would imply by monotonicity that for all
\varepsilon_{1}>1
there exists a
\varepsilon_{1}
-simulation relation and a weight function
\Delta_{a}^{1}

such that for any
\varepsilon_{1}>\varepsilon_{2}>1
there exists a
\varepsilon_{2}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
,
in which case we are also done.

If this is not the case, then there must exist some
\varepsilon_{1}>\varepsilon_{2}>1
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\not\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}

for any
\varepsilon_{2}
-simulation relations
R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
.
However, we know that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon_{2}}s_{2}
,
so there exists a
\varepsilon_{2}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
and a weight function
\Delta_{a}^{2}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
.
Note that we must have
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\neq\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})

because we have
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\not\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
but
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{2}}
.
If
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})=\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})
, we are done.
If not, it may be the case that for any
\varepsilon_{2}>\varepsilon_{3}>1

there exists a
\varepsilon_{3}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
.
This would by monotonicity again imply that we are done.

If this is not the case, then there must exist some
\varepsilon_{2}>\varepsilon_{3}>1

such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\not\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
for all
\varepsilon_{3}
-simulation relations
R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
.
However, we know that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon_{3}}s_{2}
,
so there exists a
\varepsilon_{3}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
and a weight function
\Delta_{a}^{3}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
.
Note that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2})\neq\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3})
and also
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1})\neq\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3})

since
R_{\varepsilon_{3}}
is also a
\varepsilon_{2}
-simulation relation.
If
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})=\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3})
, we are done.
If not, it may be the case that for any
\varepsilon_{3}>\varepsilon_{4}>1

there exists a
\varepsilon_{4}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon_{4}}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon_{4}}

which, by monotonicity, would imply that we are done.

Continuing in this way, we get a sequence

\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{1}),\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{2}),\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a}^{3}),\dots,

all pairwise different from each other.
However, since

\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})>0\quad\text{implies}\quad\tau(s_{1},a)(s)>0\text{ and }\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})>0,

\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})
seen as a function of
\Delta_{a}
can only take on finitely many values.
Hence the process must eventually stop and we find witnesses for the statement of the lemma.
∎

D.7.5
.

s_{1}\precsim s_{2}\quad\text{implies}\quad d(s_{1},s_{2})=1.

For finitely supported SMDPs, it also holds that

d(s_{1},s_{2})=1\quad\text{implies}\quad s_{1}\precsim s_{2}.

Proof.

The first point is immediate: If
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
, this means that
s_{1}\precsim_{1}s_{2}
, so
d(s_{1},s_{2})=1
.

For the second point, assume that
d(s_{1},s_{2})=1
.
This means that either
s_{1}\precsim_{1}s_{2}
, in which case we are done,
or
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
for all
\varepsilon>1
,
in which case we wish to prove that this implies that

s_{1}\models\varphi\implies s_{2}\models\varphi\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}.

(D.3)

This would imply, by Theorem
D.7.1
,
that
s_{1}\precsim s_{2}
, and we are done.
Hence we now prove the claim in (
D.3
) by induction on
\varphi
.

(
\varphi=\alpha
or
\varphi=\neg\alpha
):
Choose some
\varepsilon>1
.
Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R

such that
s_{1}Rs_{2}
.
This implies that
L(s_{1})=L(s_{2})
,
so if
s_{1}\models\varphi
,
then also
s_{2}\models\varphi
.

(
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
):
If
s_{1}\models\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
,
then
s_{1}\models\varphi_{1}
or
s_{1}\models\varphi_{2}
.
By induction hypothesis, this implies that
s_{2}\models\varphi_{1}
or
s_{2}\models\varphi_{2}
,
so
s_{2}\models\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
.

(
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
):
Similar to the case
\varphi=\varphi_{1}
.

(
\varphi=\ell_{p}t
):
Assume
s_{1}\models\ell_{p}t
,
which means that
F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq p
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
F_{s_{2}}(t)<p
,
and let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=p-F_{s_{2}}(t)>0
.
Then there exists
\delta>0
such that for any
t<x<t+\delta

we have
F_{s_{2}}(x)-F_{s_{2}}(t)<\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
If
t=0
then for any
\varepsilon>0

we have

p>F_{s_{2}}(t)=F_{s_{2}}(0)=F_{s_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq p,

which is a contradiction.
If
t>0
, then choose an
\varepsilon>0
such that

1<\varepsilon<\frac{t+\delta}{t}
,
meaning that
t<\varepsilon\cdot t<t+\delta
.
By right-continuity, this implies that
F_{s_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)-F_{s_{2}}(t)<\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
Hence we get

p>F_{s_{2}}(t)\geq F_{s_{2}}(\varepsilon\cdot t)\geq F_{s_{1}}(t)\geq p,

which is also a contradiction.

(
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
):
Assume
s_{1}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
, meaning that
\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p
,
and choose some
\varepsilon>1
.
By Lemma
D.7.4
,
there exists a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon}

and a coupling
\Delta_{a}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon}
such that
for any
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>1

there exists a
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
.
We then get

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\leq\tau(s_{1},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{1},a)(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,s^{\prime})\in(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket\times S)\cap\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime}).

Now, we know that for any
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>1

there exists a
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-simulation relation
R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})\subseteq R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
This means that for any
(s,s^{\prime})\in(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket\times S)\cap\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})

we have
s\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s^{\prime}
.
By monotonicity, we therefore get that
s\precsim_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}s^{\prime}
for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}>1
.
The induction hypothesis then gives

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{(s,s^{\prime})\in(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket\times S)\cap\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,s^{\prime})\in(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket\times\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\cap\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{a})}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\in S}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau(s_{2},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket),

which implies that
s_{2}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.
∎

Reachability Properties

We will now argue that the simulation distance
behaves nicely also with respect to linear-time properties,
by proving preservation of reachability properties up to perturbations.

The probability of reaching a given set of states in an SMDP depends on the
choice of actions in each state.
The non-determinism introduced by this choice is typically
resolved by means of
schedulers
.
Here we consider probabilistic schedulers
\sigma
of type
S^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A)
,
telling us what the probability is of selecting an action
a\in A
