depending on the history of the states visited so far.

Given a SMDP
M=(S,\tau,\rho,L)
,
a
path
in
M
is a sequence

\pi=(s_{1},t_{1}),(s_{2},t_{2}),\dots,

where
s_{i}\in S
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Intuitively, a path
\pi
denotes an execution of the SMDP,
where
s_{i}
denotes the
i
th state visited,
and
t_{i}
denotes the time spent in
s_{i}
.
We denote by
\Pi(M)
the set of all paths in
M
,
and we let
\pi[i]=s_{i}
and
\pi\langle i\rangle=t_{i}
.

Let
X\subseteq S
. Then

\Diamond^{t}X=\{\pi\in\Pi(M)\mid\exists i\in\mathbb{N}.\pi[i]\in X\text{ and }\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\pi\langle j\rangle\leq t\}

is the set of paths that eventually reach a state in
X

and does so within time
t
.

Given a scheduler
\sigma
, we define a probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(S_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\tau^{\sigma}(s,a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\rho(s)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\tau^{\sigma}(s,a)(s^{\prime})\cdot(\rho(s)*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s^{\prime}}(S_{2},\dots,S_{n}))

through the usual cylinder construction.
Then
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(S_{1},\dots,S_{n})(t)

is the probability, starting from
s
and under the scheduler
\sigma
,
to first visit a state in
S_{1}
,
then a state in
S_{2}
, and so on, until a state in
S_{n}
is reached,
and the total time elapsed is at most
t
.

D.7.6
.

Let
\beta
be a Boolean combination of atomic propositions.
If
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
then for any scheduler
\sigma
there exists a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}

such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)\leq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(\varepsilon\cdot t)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a
\varepsilon
-simulation relation
witnessing that
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
.

Case
n=1
:

For each
a\in A
there exists a coupling
\Delta_{a}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\tau(s_{1},a)(s)\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t).

If
s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
and
s^{\prime}\notin\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
,
then
s\not\precsim_{\varepsilon}s^{\prime}
,
and hence
(s,s^{\prime})\notin R
, so
\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})=0
.
We therefore get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\sum_{s\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{1})(t).

Now we define
\sigma^{\prime}(s_{2})(a)=\sigma(s_{1})(a)

and observe that
\rho(s_{1})(t)\leq\rho(s_{2})(\varepsilon\cdot t)

since we have assumed
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
.
Hence we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(s_{2})(a)\cdot\rho(s_{2})(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(\varepsilon\cdot t).

Case
n>1
:

For any
a\in A
we again get a coupling
\Delta_{a}
such that

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}\tau(s_{1},a)(s)\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot(\rho(s_{1})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot(\rho(s_{1})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(s_{1})(a)\cdot(\rho(s_{1})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(t).

Since
s\not\precsim_{\varepsilon}s^{\prime}
implies
\Delta_{a}(s,s^{\prime})=0
,
any term where
s\not\precsim_{\varepsilon}s^{\prime}
contributes nothing to the sum.
Hence we may assume that
s\precsim_{\varepsilon}s^{\prime}
.
By induction hypothesis, we then get that for any
s^{\prime}
there exists
\sigma^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)\leq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}_{s^{\prime}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(\varepsilon\cdot t).

Now let
w\in S^{*}
and define

\sigma^{\prime\prime}(s^{\prime}w)(a)=\sigma^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}(w)(a)\quad\text{and}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(s_{2})(a)=\sigma(s_{1})(a).

By Proposition
D.3.8
we get

(\rho(s_{1})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(t)\leq(\rho(s_{2})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}_{s^{\prime}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(\varepsilon\cdot t).

Hence we get

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}\tau(s_{2},a)(s^{\prime})\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}(s_{2})(a)\cdot(\rho(s_{2})*\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-2\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket))(\varepsilon\cdot t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(\varepsilon\cdot t).\qed

Given our notion of
\varepsilon
-simulation, we can prove the following result.

D.7.7
.

Let
\beta
be a Boolean combination of atomic propositions.
If we have
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
, then
for any scheduler
\sigma
there exists a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\Diamond^{t}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)\leq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\Diamond^{\varepsilon\cdot t}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)

\displaystyle(\text{or equivalently, }\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\neg\Diamond^{t}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)\geq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\neg\Diamond^{\varepsilon\cdot t}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)).

Proof.

First note that for any
s
and
\sigma
,
we have

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\Diamond^{t}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n}),

where

\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n}=\{\pi\in\Pi(M)\mid\pi[n]\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket,\forall k<n.\pi[k]\notin\llbracket\beta\rrbracket,\text{ and }\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\pi\langle j\rangle\leq t\}.

We will now argue that for any
\sigma
there exists
\sigma^{\prime}
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n})\leq\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{\varepsilon\cdot t}_{n})

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Case
n=1
:

In this case we have
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n})=\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}(s_{1})

and
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{\varepsilon\cdot t}_{n})=\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket}(s_{2})
.
Since
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
, we get
s_{1}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
if and only if
s_{2}\in\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
,
and hence
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n})=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{\varepsilon\cdot t}_{n})

for any
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Case
n>1
:

In this case we have

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{t}_{n})=\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}(s_{1})\cdot\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(t)

and

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{\varepsilon\cdot t}_{n})=\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}(s_{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{2}}(\underbrace{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c},\dots,\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}_{n-1\text{ times}},\llbracket\beta\rrbracket)(\varepsilon\cdot t).

Since
s_{1}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s_{2}
,
we have
\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}(s_{1})=\mathbbm{1}_{\llbracket\beta\rrbracket^{c}}(s_{2})
.
The result then follows from Lemma
D.7.6
.
∎

Note that the above result might find useful applications for speeding up the computation
time required by model checking tools to disprove certain types of reachability properties.
For example, consider the atomic proposition
\mathsf{bad}
,
identifying all the states considered “not safe” in the SMDP.
Usually, given a process
s
, one wants to verify that,
under all possible schedulers
\sigma
,
the probability
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\neg\Diamond^{t}\llbracket\mathsf{bad}\rrbracket)

is above a certain threshold value
\delta\leq 1
,
meaning that the SMDP is unlikely to end up
in an unsafe configuration within a time horizon bounded by
t
.
Then, to disprove this property one only needs to provide a scheduler

\sigma^{\prime}
and a process
s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s

and
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(\neg\Diamond^{\frac{t}{\varepsilon}}\llbracket\mathsf{bad}\rrbracket)<\delta
.
Indeed, given that
s^{\prime}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s
, by Theorem
D.7.7

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}\left(\neg\Diamond^{\frac{t}{\varepsilon}}\llbracket\mathsf{bad}\rrbracket\right)<\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Th.\ref{thm:linear}}}{{\implies}}\exists\sigma.\;\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}_{s}(\neg\Diamond^{t}\llbracket\mathsf{bad}\rrbracket)<\delta.

Since
s
simulates
s^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime}
can be thought of as a simplified abstraction of
s
,
which is usually a smaller process.
Hence, finding a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}
for
s^{\prime}
which gives a counterexample
may be much simpler than finding one for
s
.
Moreover, the above technique is robust to perturbations of
\varepsilon
.

The Topology of TML

A common practice in science and engineering is that of approximating and refining models.
We would therefore like to ensure that whenever we make better and better approximations
of a model, whatever property holds for the approximations should also hold
for the model that is approximated.
In our terms, this means that we would like the sets
satisfying formulas in
\mathrm{TML}
to be
closed
,
because then we would know that whenever a sequence of states
\{s_{n}\}

which converges to some state
s
satisfies some property of
\mathrm{TML}
,
then
s
also satisfies that property.

Since the concept of open and closed sets are topological concepts,
we introduce the topology generated by our distance.
Note that the concept of closed set and sequentially closed set
need not coincide in arbitrary topological spaces.
However, they do coincide for hemimetric spaces,
since these are first-countable
[
goubault2013
]
.
Moreover, hemimetric spaces are not in general Hausdorff,
so limits need not be unique.

Because the distance is non-symmetric,
we can in fact generate two different topologies.
For
r>1
, the open balls of the form

\mathcal{B}_{r}^{L}(s)=\{s^{\prime}\mid d(s,s^{\prime})<r\}

generate the
left-centered topology
and open balls of the form

\mathcal{B}_{r}^{R}(s)=\{s^{\prime}\mid d(s^{\prime},s)<r\}

generate the
right-centered topology
.
These two topologies behave differently, as we will now show.

D.8.1
.

The following holds in the right-centered topology.

1.

\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
is closed.

2.

If
p=0
, then
\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
is open.

3.

If
p>0
, then
\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
is not open.

4.

If
p=1
, then
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is closed.

5.

If
p<1
, then
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is not closed.

Proof.

1.

Let
\{s_{k}\}
be a sequence of states such that
s_{k}\in\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
for all
k

and
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s
. We must show that
s\in\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
s\notin\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket

and let
\varepsilon>1
.
First note that if
t=0
, then
F_{s_{k}}(0)\geq p
for all
k
.
Since
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s
,
there must exist some
n
such that
d(s_{n},s)<\varepsilon
.
But then

p\leq F_{s_{n}}(0)\leq F_{s}(\varepsilon\cdot 0)=F_{s}(0)<p,

which is a contradiction.

We can therefore now assume that
t>0
.
Let
p-F_{s}(t)=\varepsilon>0
.
By right-continuity, there exists a
\delta>0
such that

x<c<x+\delta
implies
|F_{s}(x)-F_{s}(c)|<\varepsilon
.
Now choose
\varepsilon^{\prime}
such that
1<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\frac{t+\delta}{t}
,
which means that
t<\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t<t+\delta
.
Then we get

|F_{s}(t)-F_{s}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)|<\varepsilon

which implies
F_{s}(t)\leq F_{s}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<p
.
However, since
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s
,
we know that there must exist some
n
such that

p\leq F_{s_{n}}(t)\leq F_{s}(\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot t)<p,

which is a contradiction.

2.

If
p=0
, then
F_{s}(t)\geq p
for any
s
,
so
s\in\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
for any
s
.

3.

If
p>0
, let
F_{s}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,t\right]
and
F_{s^{\prime}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[t,b\right]

for some
a
and
b
(if
t=0
,
F_{s}=\delta_{0}
instead).
Then
F_{s}(t)=1\geq p
, so
s\in\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
,
but
F_{s^{\prime}}(t)=0<p
, and hence
s^{\prime}\notin\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
.
However, for any
r>1
, we must have
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{r}(u)
,
since
F_{s}(t)\geq F_{s^{\prime}}(t)
for any
t
.
Hence
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{r}(s)\not\subseteq\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
for any
r
.

4.

If
p=1
, then
F_{s}(t)\leq p
for any
s
,
so
s\in\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
for any
s
.

5.

If
p<1
, let
s
be a state with
F_{s}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]

and let
\{s_{k}\}
be a sequence of states such that

F_{s_{k}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[b,c\right]
for any
k
.
Then
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s

and
F_{s_{k}}(b)=0\leq p
, so
s_{k}\in\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
.
However,
p<1=F_{s}(b)
, so
u\notin\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
. ∎

D.8.2
.

The following holds in the left-centered topology.

1.

If
p=1
, then
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is open.

2.

If
p<1
, then
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is not open.

3.

If
p=0
,
\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
is closed.

4.

If
p>0
,
\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
is not closed.

Proof.

1.

If
p=1
, then
F_{s}(t)\leq p
for any
s
.

2.

If
p<1
, then let
F_{s^{\prime}}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,t\right]

and
F_{s}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[t,b\right]
for some
a
and
b

(if
t=0
, let
F_{s^{\prime}}=\delta_{0}
instead).
Then
F_{s}(t)=0\leq p
and hence
s\in\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
,
but
F_{s^{\prime}}(t)=1>p
, so
s^{\prime}\notin\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
.
However,
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{L}_{r}(u)
for any
r>1

because
F_{s^{\prime}}(t)\geq F_{s}(t)
for all
t
.
Hence
\mathcal{B}^{L}_{r}(s)\not\subseteq\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
for any
r
.

3.

If
p=0
, then
F_{s}(t)\geq p
for any
s
.

4.

If
p>0
, let
s
be a state such that
F_{s}=\mathit{Unif}\!\left[b,c\right]

and let
\{s_{k}\}
be a sequence of states such that
F_{s_{k}}\sim\mathit{Unif}\!\left[a,b\right]
for any
k
.
Then
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s

and
F_{s_{k}}(b)=1\geq p
, and hence
s_{k}\in\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
for any
k
.
However,
p>0=F_{s}(b)
, so
s\notin\llbracket\ell_{p}t\rrbracket
. ∎

The case of
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is missing in Lemma
D.8.2
.
We have not been able to determine whether
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is closed in the left-centered topology,
but we strongly suspect that this is the case.
Hence we have the following conjecture.

D.8.3
.

In the left-centered topology,
\llbracket m_{p}t\rrbracket
is closed.

We can now show that approximate reasoning in
\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
is sound
with respect to the right-centered topology,
in the sense that if we have a sequence of states
s_{1},s_{2},\dots

that approximate some state
s
better and better,
then if a property holds for all
s_{1},s_{2},\dots
,
it will also hold for
s
.

D.8.4
.

For any
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
is closed in the right-centered topology.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\geq}
.
We prove by induction on
\varphi
that

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
is closed in the right-centered topology.

Case
\varphi=\ell_{p}t
: This follows by Lemma
D.8.1
.

Case
\varphi=L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
: Let
\{s_{k}\}
be a sequence
of states such that
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}\ni s

and
s_{k}\models L_{p}^{a}\varphi^{\prime}
.
Let
\varepsilon>1
. Then there exists
k^{\prime}
such that
s_{k^{\prime}}\precsim_{\varepsilon}s
,
and by assumption,
\tau(s_{k^{\prime}},a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p
.
By Theorem
D.7.1
, this implies
\tau(s,a)(\llbracket(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket)\geq p
.
Since this holds for any
\varepsilon>1
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 1}(\varphi^{\prime})_{\varepsilon}=\varphi^{\prime}
,
we then get

\tau(s,a)(\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\rrbracket)\geq p.

Case
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
: Since a finite intersection of closed sets
is again closed, we get that

\llbracket\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket

is closed by the induction hypothesis.

Case
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
: Since a finite union of closed sets is again closed,
we get that

\llbracket\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket

is closed by the induction hypothesis.
∎

Furthermore, if we assume Conjecture
D.8.3
,
then we can use a symmetric argument as in the proof of Theorem
D.8.4

to show that approximate reasoning in
\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}
is sound with respect to the left-centered topology.

D.8.5
.

For any
\varphi\in\mathrm{TML}^{\leq}
,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
is closed in the left-centered topology.

Conclusion and Open Problems

We have proposed a quantitative extension of the notion of simulation relation on SMDPs,
called
\varepsilon
-simulation, comparing the relative speed of
different processes. This quantitative notion of simulation relation induces a multiplicative
hemimetric, which we call simulation distance, measuring the least acceleration factor
needed by a process to speed up its actions in order to behave at least as fast as
another process.

We have given an efficient algorithm to compute the simulation distance
and identified a class of distributions for which the algorithm works on
finite SMDPs.
Furthermore, we have shown that, under mild conditions on the composition of
residence-time distributions on states, a generalised version of CSP-like parallel composition
on SMDPs is non-expansive with respect to this distance, showing that our distance
is suitable for compositional reasoning.
We have also shown the connection between our distance
and properties expressible in a timed extension of Markovian logic.
Namely, we have shown
that if the simulation distance between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
is at most
\varepsilon
,
then
s_{1}
satisfies the
\varepsilon
-perturbation of any property that
s_{2}
satisfies.
This result also gives a novel logical characterisation of simulation and bisimulation
for semi-Markov decision processes.
Lastly we have investigated the topological properties of our distance for this logic,
and shown that approximate reasoning is sound in the limit.

Instead of using the usual stochastic order to relate the timing behaviour of states as we have done,
one could also consider many other kinds of stochastic orders,
for example ones that compare the expected value of the distributions.
This may be more natural for applications where one wants
to consider an exponential distribution with a high enough rate to be faster than a uniform distribution.

We have shown that the timing distributions that are obtained
when composing systems are compatible with the algorithm for computing the distance
only in the case when composing systems either on the left or on the right.
A more general result showing that this also
happens when composing on both sides an arbitrary number of components
seems difficult. Nonetheless, we are confident that such a result can be obtained
for any concrete case involving common types of distributions
used in the literature.
